#3033. Did Christopher Nolan Just Bring 4K Blu-Ray Discs Back to Stores?

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The Odyssey hit the shelves of Best Buy, just in time for the holidays.

Physical media lovers, take note: Best Buy has begun selling 4K Ultra HD Blu-ray discs in-store and online. If you’ve been paying attention, you’ll know how big a pivot this is for the electronics retailer. Back in 2023, the company announced it would cease all sales of DVDs and Blu-rays entirely.

Now, thanks to Christopher Nolan’s blockbuster phenom and Oscar frontrunner The Odyssey, which is the first-ever movie to be filmed entirely on IMAX cameras, the company is once again stocking its shelves with Blu-rays. Right now, you’ll find only copies of the Greek epic in store (with a small selection of other titles available for purchase directly through the retailer’s online store), and that makes sense considering how popular it was in theaters and how awesome it will look on big TVs at home.

But this move also raises the question: Will other 4K and standard Blu-rays be coming to Best Buy stores in the near future?

A Best Buy spokesperson didn’t immediately reply to our request for comment.

When the retailer first announced it would be ditching DVD and Blu-ray sales, it cited the decades-long decline in physical media sales — which fell from its peak revenue benchmark of $16 billion in 2005 to less than $1 billion in 2024 — and planned to populate the store space with other tech-based products.

The Odyssey, the nearly 3-hour-long big-screen adaptation of Homer’s epic poem, follows King Odysseus (played by Matt Damon) as he leads his soldiers on a 10-year journey home after the Trojan War. It features a stacked slate of acting talent from Anne Hathaway and Tom Holland to Zendaya, Jon Bernthal and Charlize Theron, and earned $1.8 billion at the global box office.

Those details, along with the timing of the coming holiday shopping season, have apparently inspired Best Buy to change course.

It should be noted that physical media still has value for many people. In an era when movies and TV shows (purchased or otherwise) are suddenly pulled from streamers, the allure of owning titles on DVD and Blu-ray endures. Not to mention, 4K Blu-rays offer better picture quality than their compressed, streaming versions.

#3032. 3 Investments to Give a Child This Holiday Season and Leave Alone Until 2040

Which holdings can stay untouched for 14 years without anybody watching them?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:38PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vanguard S&P 500 ETF ›

A few shares of stock can be a better holiday gift than most toys, partly because a child won't think about them for a long time. A gift given this season and left alone until 2040 has around 14 years to compound.

The past 14 years show what that can look like. A $1,000 gift of the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.28%) at the end of November 2012 would be worth almost $6,700 as I write, with dividends reinvested. The same $1,000 in Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -1.71%)(BRKB -2.07%) Class B shares would've reached around $6,700, too. In Amazon (AMZN +3.82%), it'd be worth about $20,500.

No one should count on a repeat, especially from Amazon. But these are still the three investments I'd choose for a child now, with the index fund as the anchor. (The shares belong to the child, but gifts like this normally sit in a custodial account an adult manages until the child comes of age.)

The Vanguard S&P 500 ETF holds 506 stocks, pretty much the full S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.23%) index of big U.S. companies. Its expense ratio is 0.03%, or $0.30 each year for every $1,000 invested.

For a gift no one plans to watch, the fund's best feature might be that it updates itself. Companies that shrink tend to fall out of the index and are replaced by growing ones, and the fund handles those trades itself, so its owner doesn't have to.

Of course, that also means the fund tilts heavily toward what's largest today. Information technology stocks were around 37% of it at the end of October, so a long slump in tech could hold it back.

Berkshire Hathaway owns the BNSF railroad, a big utility business, and a lot of manufacturing, service, and retail companies. Its insurers also had almost $179 billion of float (premiums collected before claims are paid) as of Sept. 30.

Showing how steady the non-insurance side has been, Berkshire's railroad, energy, and manufacturing, service, and retailing businesses together earned $7.4 billion in the third quarter, 13% more than a year earlier. Their nine-month earnings rose 13%, too. Insurance underwriting earnings, though, dropped 43% in the quarter, and investment income dipped as well, so total operating earnings declined around 6%.

Berkshire hasn't declared a cash dividend since 1967. Instead, it reinvests in its businesses and buys back shares -- around $14.4 billion worth through September. And it still had more than $350 billion in cash and short-term Treasury bills, over a quarter of its market value.

Of course, Warren Buffett won't make the big calls over the next 14 years. Greg Abel is CEO, and Buffett became chairman emeritus in September. How Abel spends that cash might matter more than anything else.

Amazon offers the most growth of the three. Its third-quarter operating income surged 61% year over year to $28.1 billion, and its operating margin of 13.9% was its highest ever. Its cloud unit, Amazon Web Services (AWS), made up $17.8 billion of that, as sales growth accelerated to 39%.

"There has never been a trillion dollar revenue business, and we may have two of them in the next 5-10 years," CEO Andy Jassy said in Amazon's third-quarter earnings release.

Getting there is expensive. Operating cash flow for the last 12 months climbed 29% to $168.3 billion. But trailing-12-month free cash flow dropped from positive $14.8 billion a year ago to negative $7.6 billion as of June, then to negative $42.4 billion as of September, as artificial intelligence (AI) spending piled up.

In short, Amazon is taking in more cash than ever and spending even more. If that spending earns a weak return, the stock might struggle for a long time.

At about 24 times its 2027 earnings estimate, Amazon stock isn't a bargain. But I think it's a fair price for two big growth engines.

I'd make the index fund the largest part -- maybe half the gift, with the rest split between Berkshire and Amazon. The fund spreads the money among hundreds of businesses, so a setback at either company wouldn't ruin the whole gift.

All three will probably drop together in the next bear market. But a gift put aside for a child is less likely to be sold in a panic, which can matter a lot over 14 years. I'd give all three this season.

#3031. You can use Google’s Spark AI agent, renamed Tasks, for free now – with limits

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

At least if Tasks disappoints, it won't cost you - unlike OpenAI and Anthropic's subscription-based agents.

Just months after its release, Google is making its Spark agent free and officially renaming its capabilities “Tasks.” You can now toggle to it from the standard Gemini Chat setting. Reports show the rebrand started gradually in mid-October.

Also: Ready for a Siri AI agent? Apple under pressure to upgrade its assistant faster

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

If Tasks sounds familiar, it should: The company already has a product called Google Tasks, which lets you turn emails into a checkable to-do list on the side of your inbox, among other places like Chat and Docs. This is the agentic version, obviously.

Gewirtz, ZDNET’s AI tester-in-residence, wasn’t blown away by then-Spark when he tried it last month, but had reason to believe in its eventual improvement. It’s unclear whether Tasks is meaningfully different from Spark, aside from being out of beta.

Abilities aside, what sets Tasks apart here is cost. Dots and Duet haven’t exactly impressed thus far, so free alternatives don’t have a high bar to reach yet.

Also: I tested OpenAI Dot and Anthropic Duet for weeks

During Gewirtz’s test, when the agent was part of his $20 per month Google AI Pro plan, he used only 20% of the total five hours of usage on “four fairly substantial tests.” Without an AI plan, Gemini’s context window is 32k tokens.

At launch in September, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg said Muse is “free to use for up to 100M tokens per week,” though Meta’s help center is not so explicit. As with most agentic products, more frequent users may want to buy more access, but more casual users now have two options to compare.

Also: Don’t use Muse? How Meta’s AI agent can still access your private messages

As with other agents, Tasks, which runs on its newest Gemini 4 Hydrogen model, can handle a variety of jobs for you, from grocery shopping to booking appointments and analyzing your heating bill. Agents like these are designed for a chronically overwhelmed society trained by a reliance on tech to seek convenience and productivity in nearly every aspect of life.

The feature is free in the US, Canada, Korea, Japan, Australia, and India. US users can also start tasks by emailing or texting Gemini, and can have the agent call businesses for them (except in Indiana, Louisiana, Minnesota, Montana, and Nebraska).

The change from Spark to Tasks comes just a day after Google retired Gems (which let you tailor Gemini to specific tasks), and converted existing Gems into Skills. The new landing page for Tasks emphasizes personal finance and cost-cutting as a use case, positioning itself alongside personal finance features from Anthropic and OpenAI.

“Put Gemini on the hunt for ways to save. Identify recurring expenses you no longer need, get alerts for your most-desired deals, and more,” the page reads.

Also: Want to try ChatGPT Wallet? Read OpenAI’s liability disclaimer

“You can adjust task settings, disconnect Google and third-party apps, or delete chat history anytime,’ Google’s blog on the release said. Google’s new Memory summary feature for Gemini lets users view and edit the personal info the chatbot has on them.

#3030. Critical Java Flaw Leaves Systems Open to Hackers

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech > Services & Software > Cybersecurity

The bug could allow hackers to take over systems without a password.

A newly identified vulnerability in Oracle Java could give hackers unauthorized access to major applications and allow them to run code without logging in. Major tech companies like Google, Amazon, Microsoft, Apple and Netflix, which rely heavily on Java’s server-side software and core products, could be affected.

The flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-114938 (and dubbed Java4Shell), corrupts memory when Java decodes Base64, a standard format used to transmit login credentials and file uploads.

Thai Duong, the CEO of cybersecurity firm Calif, tells CNET that his company identified the critical software vulnerability in October and notified Oracle, which has since patched the vulnerability. Now, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency is urging organizations to do the same.

Tech giants like Google, Amazon, Microsoft, Apple and Netflix rely heavily on Java.

“Roughly 90% of Fortune 500 companies run on Java,” Duong said.

Oracle rated the flaw a serious 8.1 out of 10 on the standard vulnerability scale. It can lead to an unauthenticated remote code execution vulnerability in Java, allowing hackers to run their own malicious commands or code over the internet without requiring a username, password or prior access.

Federico Kirschbaum, head of Security Lab, the research arm at cybersecurity company XBOW, tells CNET that Java is primarily used at the enterprise level. As a result, the vulnerability could trickle across different sectors, including finance, government, transportation and large corporations.

“Java is a great language for creating solutions, but this vulnerability — it’s a reminder that maybe the languages that we’re using have flaws, and everything built on those might inherit that problem,” Kirschbaum said.

Amid recent technological disruptions, he suggests leaning on AI solutions to address vulnerabilities in AI.

“That new normal is that most of the technology we rely on has flaws, and now that we have resources using LLMs, we can find those problems,” Kirschbaum said.

He points out that bad actors and hackers can also find these vulnerabilities.

“We are in a race to find the problems first and help the industry by guiding them on what’s urgent so they patch it,” Kirschbaum said.

Since Calif discovered Java4Shell in October, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency has issued guidance urging organizations to patch it immediately.

The agency recommends identifying affected Java installations, reviewing software inventory for JDK’s and Java runtimes and applying relevant vendor patches as soon as they’re available.

#3029. 5 new Apple Health features you should try in iOS 27.3 – if your phone qualifies

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Apple's redesigned Health app arrives in December with some major upgrades, but not every iPhone will get the full experience.

Apple is kicking off a new and improved Health app next month with the release of iOS 27.3. Instead of just recording and displaying your health-related stats, the app will tap into Apple Intelligence to analyze the data and dispense advice on how to improve your health and fitness. But that reliance on AI means you’ll need the right type of iPhone to enjoy all the benefits.

Also: Apple could build its AI health chatbot from this study – how to enroll

Here are five helpful new features built into the app, along with what you’ll need to access them.

First up, the app will offer suggestions based on data from different sources. For example, it might advise you to go for a walk at a nearby park if it detects that you’re not getting enough exercise. It may show you articles and videos about sleep if it finds that you’re not well-rested. It could also highlight possible conditions based on your lab and test results. You can easily view these recommendations from the Insights screen.

Also: Health is Tim Cook’s defining legacy – and your Apple Watch proves it

A new feature called Health Age compares your physical health to your actual age to show how you’re doing. The analysis draws on certain stats and lab data, such as your resting heart rate, sleep, heart rate variability (HRV), cholesterol, and A1C (average blood glucose levels), to deliver a health age, which may differ from your chronological age. The results can show you areas that may be of concern or that you need to focus on.

Also: Apple Watch Series 12 review: Finally, the health tracker I’ve been waiting for

With a new feature called Movement Evaluations and an iPhone and Apple Watch, the Health app can assess your flexibility, strength, balance, VO2 max (maximum oxygen absorption), and overall movement. You perform a physical routine while your iPhone “watches” you via the camera. Using built-in AI, the app will provide audio and visual feedback in real-time to tell you how you’re faring.

Apple promises that the analysis is performed on your device without recording, storing, or sharing the video.

The new app will be able to correlate your test results with data collected by your iPhone and Apple Watch to give you a more comprehensive picture of your health. Plus, Apple and Quest Diagnostics have teamed up to offer people in the US an optional $119 bloodwork panel that covers more than 50 biomarkers to scan for different health and medical conditions.

Also: I tested the Apple Watch Series 12 heart-rate claims against my gold-standard Polar strap

Normally, you need an Apple Fitness+ subscription to fire up workout videos to help you exercise. But the new Health app will come with its own comprehensive set of training videos. Available from the Insights section, a series of strength exercise videos are designed to help you build your body strength. The videos cover all the key areas of your body to give you a full workout.

Some of the new features, such as the strength videos, will be available to all iPhone users. But the ones that rely on AI will run only on phones that support Apple Intelligence. That means you’ll need an iPhone 15 Pro or Pro Max, any iPhone 16 or 17, an iPhone 18 Pro or Pro Max, an iPhone Air, or an iPhone Duo.

#3028. Voyager 1 Becomes the First Spacecraft to Travel a Full Light-Day From Earth

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Voyager 1 has been flying at about 38,000 mph since 1980. That steady pace carried it a full light-day from home.

NASA’s Voyager 1 left Earth on Sept. 5, 1977, carrying a camera, a set of science instruments and a gold-plated record of sounds and images from home. Forty-nine years later, it’s reached a distance no spacecraft has attained before: one light-day, or roughly 16.1 billion miles, from Earth. At that range, light needs a full 24 hours to make the trip between Earth and the probe.

At 5:16:07 a.m. ET on Wednesday, Voyager 1 became the first human-made object to reach that light-day milestone. From that point on, a radio signal between the spacecraft and NASA’s Deep Space Network takes more than a day to travel in either direction.

NASA puts the journey at 49 years, two months and 11 days, counting from Voyager 1’s launch in September 1977. The probe still sends data home, though at a rate slower than dial-up, and NASA plans a live discussion with the Voyager team on Thursday to mark the occasion.

It’s quite an accomplishment for a spacecraft built in the mid-1970s. One light-day is the distance that light travels in a single day at the speed of light, which is approximately 186,282 miles per second. That translates to 25.9 billion kilometers or about 16.1 billion miles in a single day. At an average human walking speed of 3 mph, it would take a human 612,015 years to travel that distance on foot.

Fortunately, Voyager 1 has been traveling a bit faster. It currently zips through the cosmos at a steady 37,763 mph, a speed it achieved in 1980 after a gravity-assisted Saturn flyby. It has been flying at that speed ever since.

The milestone comes 14 years after Voyager 1 crossed the heliopause, the point where the sun’s heliosphere ends, and entered interstellar space. It’s also 28 years after the spacecraft passed Pioneer 10 to become the human-made object that has traveled the farthest into space.

NASA marked the milestone with a global Shine Bright for Voyager’s Light-Day event on Tuesday evening. Just after sunset, the agency wanted people to head outside and point their smartphone or tablet lights skyward to spark a planet-wide wave of light as Earth rotates, much like fans doing the wave at a stadium.

The space agency also made a website for the occasion. The website is simple. It flashes Voyager’s VGR call sign in Morse code. If you want to try it, click the link, hit begin, and then point your phone or tablet screen at the sky. The website does all the work for you.

NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory will follow up on Thursday at noon ET with a moderated discussion on the milestone, featuring status updates on both Voyager probes and a live Q&A with reporters and audience members.

The historic milestone isn’t without its drama. Voyager 1 has been in space for nearly half a century, and in that time, the Jet Propulsion Laboratory has done everything it can to extend the spacecraft’s life. That includes troubleshooting issues, performing maintenance, and shutting down various systems to save power and extend the spacecraft’s lifespan.

Several of those efforts took place in 2026. The team running Voyager 1 spent weeks shutting down three devices and powering on three low-power alternatives to reduce overall power usage. This initiative, which was internally called the Big Bang, was also performed on Voyager 2. The transition was completed in early September, giving Voyager 1 more time with its dwindling power supply while also keeping it warm enough to continue operating in the frigid conditions of interstellar space.

It was a tricky maneuver that required the team to simultaneously turn multiple instruments on and off. The difficulty of the maneuver was amplified by the fact that it took almost a full day for a signal to reach Voyager and another full day for Voyager to send a signal back, with hours of downtime while the spacecraft performed the operations.

All of this took place several months after Voyager 1 experienced a sudden drop in power, which necessitated shutting off one of its three instruments still in operation. Voyager 1 now has only two operational instruments.

Making it a whole light-day away from Earth is likely going to be Voyager 1’s final milestone while it still has enough power to communicate with Earth. Voyager 1 is powered by three radioisotope thermoelectric generators. They are essentially nuclear-powered batteries that generate energy by converting the steady heat from decaying plutonium-238.

When Voyager 1 first launched, its radioisotope thermoelectric generators produced about 470 watts of power. But plutonium-238 doesn’t last forever, and after 50 years in space, those generators now produce about 215 watts of power, with an average loss of about 4 watts per year. Most of that power is used to keep Voyager 1 up and running, which is why NASA has shut down eight of the 10 instruments that Voyager had when it launched.

At its current rate of usage and decay, Voyager 1 is expected to shut down its final science instrument in the 2030s and will eventually lose communication with Earth in 2036 due to insufficient power to send back a radio signal.

The same fate is expected for Voyager 2, Voyager 1’s twin, which has also spent half a century in space. While it is labeled the sequel, Voyager 2 actually launched 16 days before Voyager 1, and the two have spent nearly identical amounts of time traversing the cosmos. Thanks to its slower speed and trajectory, it likely won’t reach the one light-day distance until after its instruments shut down.

Voyager 1 will lose all of its power before it hits its next milestone, which is the beginning of the Oort Cloud, a vast, icy shell of comets at the edge of our solar system. It’ll take about 300 years for Voyager 1 to make it there.

Thanks to the near-perfect vacuum of outer space, Voyager 1 will continue to fly at its current speed even after it shuts down entirely. So it’ll still hit that milestone; it just won’t be able to phone home to tell us about it.

#3027. Stock-Split Watch: Is Micron Next?

Micron looks poised for a stock split, especially after Sandisk's recent announcement.

By Marc Guberti – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:43PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Micron Technology ›

Micron Technology (MU -1.00%) may be the next tech stock to perform a stock split. Even after a lengthy correction, it still trades above $700 per share. It's normal for companies to initiate splits when they get closer to $1,000 per share, and investors already saw that unfold when fellow memory stock Sandisk (SNDK -2.64%) announced an 8-for-1 split.

While an 8-for-1 split doesn't make sense for Micron at its current price, a smaller split may make sense in the near future.

Micron's memory chips have become a vital part of artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure, and investors have seen that truth play out in recent financial results. Each time that bears question the likelihood of Micron extending its gains, the company delivers robust earnings that beat expectations and hint at more future growth.

Fiscal 2026 fourth-quarter earnings capture how this story has been playing out. Micron told investors in the previous quarter to expect $50 billion in fiscal 2026 Q4 revenue at the midpoint, but it delivered $54.2 billion instead. Beating guidance helped Micron deliver 379% year-over-year revenue growth, and it told investors that fiscal 2027 will be even better.

If Micron continues to deliver tremendous fundamental growth, its stock could rally and reclaim its all-time high above $1,200 per share. A return to that level further justifies a stock split.

Micron has been securing multiyear deals with key tech companies, making it easier to forecast future revenue. These long-term deals were one of the biggest developments that shattered the bearish narrative about inventory gluts possibly weakening Micron's pricing power in the future.

This type of boom-and-bust cycle has been the norm in the semiconductor industry, but AI demand is still going parabolic. Grand View Research projects a 30.6% compound annual growth rate (CAGR) for the artificial intelligence industry through 2033. That suggests more demand for Micron's memory chips and additional pricing power.

The growth stock trades at a measly 4.5 forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio, giving it a lower valuation than most bank stocks, which are achieving much lower growth rates. It's hard to find a stock that combines Micron's valuation and growth rates, and this one-two punch should ignite a stock rally in the months ahead.

Any rapid acceleration in Micron’s stock price, similar to what investors saw earlier this year, would provide further justification for a stock split. Sandisk initiating its own stock split may prompt Micron to follow suit.

#3026. Google Wallet comes to your iPhone – but without this feature

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > iOS

The new Google Wallet app brings Gemini agentic shopping to US-based iOS users - sorry, no tap-to-pay.

The Google Wallet app is available for iPhone users in the US for the first time. iOS users who download the new Google Wallet app can manage online payments for faster checkout at places that support Google Pay. However, Apple restricts Google’s ability to offer tap-to-pay service on the iOS app, so users won’t be able to use Google Wallet for contactless checkout on iPhone.

Also: You can use Google’s Spark AI agent, renamed Tasks, for free now – with limits

The new Google Wallet app lets you digitize physical cards, including payment, loyalty, and membership cards, as well as boarding passes. Google Wallet app users on iOS will also be able to view receipts and track orders pulled from Gmail in one app.

There used to be a Google Pay app for iOS, but it was discontinued in 2024. The old Google Pay app also didn’t support tap-to-pay on iPhone, but it offered limited functionality, including support for loyalty and gift cards, as well as purchase tracking.

The biggest difference between the old Google Pay app for iOS and the new Google Wallet is the AI component, which supports agentic shopping, likely as Google’s longer-term play.

One of Google’s goals for the Wallet app on iOS is to make it a central place to manage purchases made by its AI agents. The Agents section in Google Wallet for iPhone lets you manage and monitor purchases made through Gemini.

Agentic commerce is on the rise, as evidenced by the growing use of Meta’s Muse. Gemini can make purchases and payments through the Tasks feature, which lets you give Gemini permission to buy the items you want and set your maximum budget.

Also: Google Wallet just turned your Android phone into a digital house key

The Google Wallet app for iOS becomes the central hub for purchase management in agentic commerce through Gemini. In it, you can view active tasks to see which purchases Gemini is working on, as well as completed purchases and receipts.

Google has been working on two technologies to support its agentic commerce strategy: Universal Commerce Protocol (UCP) and Agent Payments Protocol (AP2).

UCP is a technical language that enables AI agents to communicate with retailers and make purchases, while AP2 is the payment framework that establishes rules and authorization for agents to make payments.

Google Wallet serves as both the AI-powered wallet and the consumer-facing record of transactions made by Gemini.

Also: Google Wallet has a lot of new tricks – these 5 are my favorites

Along with Google, major retailers supported the development of UCP, including Shopify, Walmart, Target, and Etsy. When Google announced AP2, more than 60 organizations became involved, including PayPal, Salesforce, Mastercard, and American Express.

#3025. Why Bullish Stock Crushed it on Wednesday

It topped analyst estimates in its latest reported quarter.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:43PM EST

The investing world was very bullish on cryptocurrency exchange operator Bullish (BLSH +5.66%) on Hump Day. That's mainly because the company posted an earnings report featuring a massive leap in headline profitability. Mr. Market rewarded this by sending Bullish's shares up by almost 6% in that trading session.

Before market open, Bullish, perhaps best known for owning the popular crypto news and analysis web portal CoinDesk, reported that its non-IFRS (adjusted) revenue in the third quarter was $84.4 million. That was up a sturdy 10% year over year, and was well above the average analyst estimate of $79.3 million.

On the bottom line, the company's net income swelled to over $576 million ($3.57 per share) from $18.5 million in the third quarter of 2025.

It was the vagaries of the crypto market that juiced Bullish's profitability. The value of its proprietary digital asset holdings flipped from an $11.4 million loss in the year-ago quarter to a $598.4 million gain. Stripping that and other one-time and unique items out of the equation, the company's adjusted net profit still recorded a gain, coming in at $18.7 million ($0.12 per share) from $13.8 million. Analysts were modeling $0.08 per share for the metric.

In the earnings release, Bullish announced the launch of a $300 million share repurchase program. It did not specify an end date for the initiative.

In the release, CEO Tom Farley signaled Bullish's strategy to grow its business via its pending acquisition. In May, the company agreed to acquire shareholder services company Equiniti in a deal valued at $4.2 billion; the transaction is expected to close this coming January. Farley aims to combine that company’s sprawling public-company shareholder registry with Bullish’s platform to offer tokenized stock trading.

I like that Bullish is active and assertive in building out its operations, and it's admirable that management is seeking ways to distinguish it in the crypto services sphere. That said, I'm not convinced tokenized securities will be a powerful motor of growth, so I'd temper my enthusiasm about its third-quarter showing.

#3024. Dividend Stocks for 2027: Kimberly-Clark vs. McDonald's

Kimberly-Clark trades at a noticeably lower multiple than McDonald's, but the franchise giant's richer price tag raises a question about which premium is justified.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 18, 2026 at 8:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Kimberly-Clark ›

Investors often seek stability in market giants that dominate daily life. Deciding between a consumer essentials powerhouse like Kimberly-Clark (KMB -0.84%) and a global restaurant titan like McDonald's (MCD -0.65%) requires weighing income against growth.

Kimberly-Clark provides essential hygiene products that consumers buy regardless of the economic climate. McDonald's leverages a massive franchise model to generate consistent cash flow through the fast-food market. Comparing these two involves looking at how defensive positioning in household goods stacks up against the high-margin, scalable nature of the global restaurant business.

Kimberly-Clark sells essential hygiene and health products to households and professionals among consumer staples stocks worldwide. Its brands, including Huggies and Kleenex, reach customers in more than 175 countries through supermarkets and e-commerce platforms. Walmart (WMT -0.13%) is its largest customer, accounting for nearly 16% of net sales, and customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $16.6 billion. The company reported net income of close to $3.3 billion during this same timeframe. Kimberly-Clark has total cash of $1.8 billion, with total debt of $6.5 billion. Over a 12-month trailing period, it has operating cash flow of $3.3 billion.

McDonald's operates a global fast-food empire by primarily franchising its locations to independent owners rather than running them directly. This model allows for high net margins compared to many traditional retail-focused businesses. It also maintains a delivery partnership with Uber Technologies (UBER -0.07%) to help boost volume and customer loyalty across more than a dozen global markets.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $27.8 billion, which shows revenue growth of nearly 5.8%. Net income for this period was roughly $9 billion, demonstrating the company's ability to turn sales into significant bottom-line profit. The steady expansion of the franchise system and delivery infrastructure supports these consistent financial results.

Kimberly-Clark faces intense competition from other branded household products and lower-priced private label manufacturers. It is sensitive to price changes in volatile commodities like cellulose fiber and petroleum-based materials used in manufacturing. Additionally, the company must manage the integration of its acquisition of Kenvue while navigating global regulatory and sustainability reporting requirements.

McDonald's deals with fierce competition and depends heavily on the financial health of its individual franchisees. Labor shortages and rising wage costs can pressure the system, while food safety concerns or public perception issues can damage the global brand reputation.

McDonald's carries a significantly higher premium than Kimberly-Clark based on both its forward price-to-earnings (P/E) and its price-to-sales (P/S) ratio, which measures market cap relative to sales over the past twelve months.

Both Kimberly-Clark and McDonald's are well-run companies, and while McDonald's is known for its singular brand, Kimberly-Clark is known for multiple brands, ranging from Kleenex to Band-Aid. Both are also dividend stocks, helping investors generate more income. Kimberly-Clark's dividend offers a higher yield of 4.7%, while McDonald's is 2.8%.

That said, between the two, I'd rather own Kimberly-Clark. That's because in tight economic times, Kimberly-Clark offers items people will keep buying, whereas people may limit how often they go out to eat. We are seeing a bit of that play out as McDonald's figures out how to keep prices lower amid rising costs. For its 2026 third-quarter results, restaurant sales in the United States decreased by 0.2%.

In comparison, Kimberly-Clark has more predictable demand, which is nice to have with all the uncertainty swirling around the markets. In the first nine months of 2026, Kimberly-Clark reported a 1% increase in net sales to $12.4 billion. That 1% increase may not sound exciting, but again, it's about consistency. Also, the acquisition of the consumer healthcare company Kenvue gives Kimberly-Clark even more essential products to sell, adding brands like Tylenol and Listerine to its portfolio. While consumers may cut back on going out to eat and ordering burgers and fries, they won't hesitate to buy Huggies or Tylenol if they need them.

#3023. 5 Student Loan Scams to Avoid in 2026

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Student loan scams use pressure tactics and make false promises. Here’s what to watch out for.

Student loan scams can be costly, but they usually leave clues. Scammers will often try to hook victims with tempting claims of “act now or miss out” and “we promise to get you student aid."

The communication might seem realistic, but unexpected fees, unknown contacts and big promises are usually red flags. The age-old adage is true: if an offer sounds too good to be true, it probably is.

Here’s a breakdown of common student loan scams and what to do about them.

» MORE: How to spot student loan repayment scams

The scam: A fraudulent private company offers to manage your student loans and help you get them relieved or even fully forgiven. These services can cost more than $1,000, and there’s no guarantee that you’ll actually get the help you’re looking for.

How to avoid it: You can manage your federal student loans for free. According to the U.S. Department of Education, you never have to pay for help with your federal student loans[1]U.S. Department of Education. Avoiding Student Aid Scams. View all sources . You can work with your loan servicer to adjust your payment plan, consolidate loans and apply for loan forgiveness at no cost to you.

If you’re being promised immediate student loan forgiveness, are asked for personal identification information like your FAFSA login credentials or asked to authorize a power of attorney, proceed with caution — it’s likely a scam.

» MORE: How to get help with student loans

The scam: A company charges you an upfront fee to combine multiple student loans into one new loan to reduce your monthly payment. The company may be illegitimate and is likely to pocket the fee without actually consolidating your loans.

How to avoid it: You can consolidate federal student loans for free. There is no fee to consolidate your loans through the Department of Education at studentaid.gov. If you’re looking to reduce your monthly payment through consolidation, start by contacting your loan servicer. And if you get unsolicited communication from a company promising federal student loan consolidation, it's probably a scam.

The scam: Scammers use transitions in federal loan programs to take advantage of borrowers. For example, the Education Department was set to resume wage garnishments after a long pause, but in January 2026 announced a temporary delay implement repayment reforms.

When federal decisions go back and forth like this, it creates a gray area for scammers to make wage garnishment threats. For borrowers who may not know if wage garnishment restarted, it can be hard to know what to do. This goes for other federal changes, too; any uncertainty can be an opportunity for bad actors to take advantage of the confusion.

How to avoid it: If your wages are truly being garnished, the communication will come from the federal government and not a private company. Likewise, if a company offers to stop your wage garnishment for a fee, you’re probably getting scammed.

The scam: Criminals impersonate a real person and apply to colleges in their name. Then, they can collect financial aid, take out loans and use their college admission to net discounts on goods and services. This is known as a “ghost student” scam.

How to avoid it: Follow the steps on identitytheft.gov to report fraud and get a recovery plan. There are a few safeguards you can put in place to prevent this form of identity theft, such as setting up a credit freeze and monitoring your FSA ID activity.

How it works: You might receive a call from someone claiming to be from your school’s financial aid office saying you have unpaid tuition. The caller may threaten that you cannot start your classes until your tuition bill is paid in full. Scammers can be creative and convincing, even going so far as to replicate a school’s phone number to display on your caller ID.

How to avoid it: The Federal Trade Commission warns that schools will not call and pressure you to pay tuition[2]Federal Trade Commission. Got a call about unpaid college tuition? It could be a scam. Accessed Nov 16, 2026. View all sources . Rather than trust the caller, use your school's secure student portal to check your account balance. Know for certain what you owe and to whom before sending money. You can also call your financial aid office to confirm the legitimacy of a phone number that has called you requesting tuition funds.

Realizing you’ve been scammed is stressful, and it’s natural to feel overwhelmed or embarrassed. But acting quickly can make a real difference. The sooner you report what happened, the better your chances of limiting the financial damage and getting your accounts back on track. Here’s what to do:

If you’ve already paid a scammer, report the problem to the bank that issued the payment. If you paid with a credit card, for instance, contact the company and tell them what happened. They may be able to freeze your account or reverse the transaction.

Reach out to your federal student loan servicer as well and report what happened. They can check to make sure no changes have been made to your account and, in the case that changes did occur, they can help you get your loan back on track.

File complaints with the Education Department, the Federal Trade Commission and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau. Filing complaints with these agencies helps hold scammers accountable, can result in enforcement action or business shutdowns, and in some cases may help you recover lost funds.

#3022. Warren Buffett Expects Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA, BRKB) Stock to Drop About 50% Again Someday. Here's What He Says to Do Then.

Berkshire's stock has already been roughly halved (or more) three times. That should give investors confidence.

By Selena Maranjian – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:05PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Berkshire Hathaway ›

When Warren Buffett talks, most savvy investors listen. Last year, in his now-annual Thanksgiving letter, he said:

Our stock price will move capriciously, occasionally falling 50% or so as has happened three times in 60 years under present management.

Buffett followed that sentence with this one:

Don't despair; America will come back and so will Berkshire shares.

Buffett was at the helm of Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -1.71%) (BRKB -2.07%) for about 60 years, and grew the company by a remarkable annual average of nearly 20% over that period. Now 96 years old, he has since stepped away from that top job and is chairman emeritus.

Buffett actually detailed the three 50%-ish drops earlier, in his 2017 letter to shareholders, along with a 37% drop. I'm including that data in the table below. Note that it refers to the company's Class A shares, not the Class B shares that most shareholders likely own.

Both classes of stock generally move in tandem. As I write this, Class A shares are trading at around $878,500 apiece, and Class B shares near $587. Few of us could afford a share of Class A stock, which is part of the reason that Class B shares exist.

There are lessons to be drawn from that table that apply to much of our investing, not just to Berkshire Hathaway stock. For starters, note how the lengths of the declines vary -- from a few weeks to a year or two.

That's a great reminder that whenever the stock market corrects or crashes -- and the S&P 500 has actually been generally declining over the past month or so, down about 3% -- it may not stay down very long. (Berkshire Class A shares, meanwhile, are up about 17.5% over the past month!)

Note, too, that Berkshire shares and stocks, in general, tend to bounce back quite well, either quickly or eventually. Look at that 50.7% drop nearly 20 years ago, from $147,000 per share to $72,400. And look where the shares are now -- $878,500.

The folks at The Hartford Funds have offered some handy insights about market downturns, noting that:

So take Buffett's advice to heart. Expect occasional downturns and plan to ride them out while seeking bargains. Only keep money in stocks that you won't need for five, if not more, years, so that you can ride out a downturn.

#3021. Survey: Gen Z Loves a Winter Challenge, but Money Goals Often Fizzle

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Gen Zers are apt to try cold-weather challenges like “winter arc.” But they’re also the generation most likely to say they generally quit challenges before finishing and set the same money goal more than once due to not sticking with it.

It’s not technically winter yet, but depending on where you live, it may feel like it. For many of us, this is a time of hibernation, but for others, it’s prime time for a glow-up.

“Winter arc” is a popular social media challenge through which people aim to improve themselves before the new year. It typically runs from October through December, but can be started any time during the cold months. The aim is to jump-start the “new year, new me” season before the ball even drops.

Whether they call it a winter arc or not, plenty of Americans are working on self-improvement as the chill sets in. According to a new NerdWallet survey, conducted online by The Harris Poll, 75% of Americans say they’re working on their physical health, and 69% say the same about their finances.

Popular as it is, winter arc isn’t the only cold-weather challenge out there. The survey found that half of Americans (50%) say they’ve tried some sort of winter improvement challenge. And no one likes a challenge like Gen Z: Nearly three-quarters of Gen Zers (74%) have tried a winter challenge, compared with 64% of millennials, 42% of Gen Xers and 29% of baby boomers.

Note: The survey defines Gen Zers as those 18-29; millennials, ages 30-45; Gen Xers, ages 46-61 and baby boomers, ages 62-80.

But there’s a difference between starting a challenge and successfully completing one. Gen Zers reign supreme as those most likely to hop on a winter challenge. But they’re also the most likely to say they generally quit challenges before finishing, buy products they saw on social media in an effort to stick with the challenges and set the same financial goal more than once.

Gen Zers are more likely than any other generation to say they:

Have tried a winter financial challenge other than no-spend January (21%, vs. 11% of millennials, 5% of Gen Xers and 2% of baby boomers).

Generally quit challenges before they finish them (59%, vs. 42% of millennials, 36% of Gen Xers and 22% of baby boomers).

Expect to spend more on at least one discretionary expense this winter than they would during another season (90%, vs. 83% of millennials, 61% of Gen Xers and 48% of baby boomers).

Put off dealing with money until after the holidays (60%, vs. 47% of millennials, 35% of Gen Xers and 15% of baby boomers).

Aren’t currently working on improving their finances, but want to make it a priority (15%, vs. 8% of millennials and 6% each of Gen Xers and baby boomers).

“Embracing the winter arc trend can be a great way to find motivation to make progress on a goal over the slower winter months,” says Kimberly Palmer, personal finance expert at NerdWallet. “Pick a winter challenge that aligns with a longer-term goal for the coming year.”

Winter challenges popular with Gen Z include diet- or fitness-related challenges (29%) and self-improvement challenges, like winter arc or 75 Hard, a mental toughness challenge that runs for 75 days and includes two workouts every day, a strict diet and daily nonfiction reading (25%).

While winter arcs typically focus on physical, mental and intellectual self-improvement, Gen Z is game to try a financial challenge as well: One long-standing financial challenge — no-spend January — is more popular among younger Americans (22% of Gen Zers and 20% of millennials) than their older counterparts (12% of Gen Xers and 6% of baby boomers). As for other types of financial challenges, Gen Z is more likely than any other generation to have tried at least one: 21% compared with 11% of millennials, 5% of Gen X and 2% of baby boomers.

Of course, it’s one thing to try a challenge, during wintertime or otherwise. It’s another thing entirely to follow through and complete it. That’s the hard part for many Gen Zers.

When taking on a self-improvement challenge, the beginning tends to be easiest. You’re motivated and excited to reach goals and better yourself. But as time goes on, it’s easy to slip back into old habits or for the shine of a brand-new challenge to dim. And Gen Zers report that they are most likely to quit early.

According to the survey, nearly 3 in 5 Gen Zers (59%) say they generally quit challenges before they finish them. That compares with 42% of millennials, 36% of Gen Xers and 22% of baby boomers.

Along with challenges in general, money goals can be difficult to follow through with, especially for younger Americans. Around two-thirds of Gen Zers (64%) say it’s been hard to stick to financial goals they’ve set in the past, compared with 52% of Gen Xers and 34% of baby boomers. Gen Zers are more likely than any other generation to say they’ve set the same financial goal more than once because they didn’t stick with it — 33%, compared with 20% of millennials, 15% of Gen Xers and 7% of baby boomers.

This isn’t for lack of desire, though. Seven in 10 Gen Zers (70%) say they’re working on improving their finances. All generations show high levels of effort here: 76% each of millennials and Gen Xers say the same, along with 59% of baby boomers.

So why is it so hard to stick with money goals? One possibility is that it’s difficult to see meaningful progress on finances, particularly when you’re young. According to the survey, 65% of Gen Zers say it’s harder to see progress on financial goals than on physical goals.

This makes perfect sense for this age group — for those light on money but rich in youthfulness, desired physical changes may show up faster than financial progress. On top of that, the current job market is tough for younger Americans. Recent college grads now have a higher unemployment rate than workers in general. Even if you’re still on the job hunt, your 20s are a great time to start taking steps, however small, toward building future wealth.» MORE: I’m 25. Here’s How I Got Started Investing

“To increase your chances of success, try coordinating winter arc plans with a friend or giving yourself smaller goals along the way,” Palmer says. “Leaning on the support or coaching from a loved one can make it easier to keep going, as can celebrating incremental progress.”

Self-improvement is a noble pursuit. But as with most things in life, it’s easy to lose sight of the ultimate goal in favor of buying things to help you reach it.

“It’s easy to feel like a quick purchase can help us meet our goals, and sometimes a product can be a useful tool, but the reality is that true winter arc progress comes from putting in the hard work. There’s no option for ‘purchasing’ it instead,” Palmer says.

According to the survey, more than a quarter of Gen Zers (26%) say they’ve bought a product they saw on social media to help with a personal goal or challenge. This is more than any other generation: 17% of millennials, 11% of Gen Xers and 7% of baby boomers say they’ve made social media buys for self-betterment.

Some self-improvement purchases may be billed on a monthly basis. More than 1 in 5 Gen Zers (23%) say they're still paying for a subscription or membership they signed up for during a past goal or challenge. This is also true for 17% of millennials, 9% of Gen Xers and 2% of baby boomers.

These challenge purchases may pile onto already higher winter expenses. A majority of Gen Zers (90%) say they expect to spend more on at least one discretionary expense this winter than they would during another season, more than any other generation. Top expenses include food delivery (38%), entertainment at home (35%) and indoor family entertainment (30%). A quarter of Gen Zers (25%) expect to spend more on fitness or wellness apps this winter compared to other seasons.

Some may be spending more to make themselves feel better during the cold, gray months. Nearly 1 in 5 Gen Zers (18%) say they tend to combat winter blues by spending money, compared with 10% of Gen Xers and 3% of baby boomers.

The holiday season is an expensive time of year for many of us, and it’s easy to sweep finances under the rug until the new year. According to the survey, 3 in 5 Gen Zers (60%) say they generally put off dealing with money until after the holidays. This is more than any other generation: 47% of millennials, 35% of Gen Xers and 15% of baby boomers say the same.

Even for those taking on financial challenges before the new year hits, it’s easy for the resulting “savings” to disappear. Nearly three-quarters of Gen Zers (74%) say money they save by cutting back in one area usually just gets spent somewhere else instead of staying “saved.” This is also true for 68% of millennials, 63% of Gen Xers and 45% of baby boomers.

For some, the holidays are just one thing competing for their money. Over half of Gen Zers (52%) say they’d rather spend money on how they look now than save it for their future financial security. This compares with 26% of Gen Xers and 13% of baby boomers.

We don’t begrudge anyone for their desire to look and feel their best this upcoming season. But that doesn’t have to mean you can’t level up your money at the same time.

More than 2 in 5 Gen Zers (43%) say they’re putting consistent work into improving their finances, and 27% are putting some work into this, but not consistently. Moreover, 15% of Gen Zers aren’t working on improving their finances but want to make them a priority. No matter your age, here are some ideas for a financial glow-up before the spring thaw.

Start small if you tend to quit. Nearly 2 in 5 Americans (38%) say they generally quit challenges before finishing them. Early-challenge motivation can make it easy to overcommit. If you often take on challenges impulsively then peter out a few weeks later, try starting small. For example, maybe no-spend January is a stretch, but you can still save by intentionally creating two no-spend days a week.

Move money the day you save it. Over 3 in 5 Americans (61%) say money they save by cutting back in one area usually gets spent somewhere else. If you’re cutting back, transfer the difference to savings that same day, so it can’t leak out elsewhere.

Make money progress measurable. Half of Americans (50%) say it’s harder to see progress on financial goals than physical goals. There’s a saying that what gets measured, gets managed. Choose one number to track. This might be down payment savings, debt payoff progress or an investment milestone. It may not move much month to month, but you’d be surprised at the difference a year can make, even without a ton of money each month to dedicate toward your goal.

Begin before January. More than a third of Americans (37%) say they put off dealing with money until after the holidays. Facing your finances during the often busy and expensive final months of the year can be daunting, but don’t put your head in the sand until after the holiday credit card balances come due. Put a cap on your winter spending before the new year to avoid going overboard during the tempting sales this time of year.

“If you’re trying to choose a winter arc, pick one that’s realistic but also a stretch goal, so it’s satisfying to accomplish while not being completely out of reach,” Palmer suggests. “Chances are you’ll be glad with what you got done over the colder months when springtime hits.”

#3020. Why Peabody Energy Stock Slumped Today

An analyst made a deep cut to his price target on the coal company.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:09PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Peabody Energy ›

Investors struggled to find the energy to be positive about Peabody Energy (BTU -1.45%) on Wednesday. An analyst tracking the storied coal company made a significant cut to his price target, which dampened sentiment toward the stock. Its shares closed the day nearly 1.5% lower.

That move came before the market open and was made by Freedom Broker's Vitaly Kononov. He now believes Peabody stock is destined for $22 per share, down considerably from his former level of $30.

Despite the deep cut, Kononov remained bullish on the coal company's future, as he maintained his buy recommendation.

According to reports, the prognosticator's new take centered around Centurion, Peabody's flagship metallurgical coal mine in Australia. Kononov noted that the production outlook for the play has been cut three times so far this year. The analyst also considers coal prices to be vulnerable.

Centurion is rightfully considered to be Peabody's most important play, so how it develops will make a major difference to the company's results. What I'd be nervous about if I were a company shareholder is the future of the energy industry, an important client base for Peabody's thermal coal business, given Peabody's continued (and deep) involvement in the segment. For numerous reasons, the energy sector has become greener over the years, and I think that's a permanent shift.

I'm bearish on coal companies generally for this reason, and as such I'm bearish on Peabody.

#3019. Mortgage Rates Today, Tuesday, November 17: A Little Higher

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates continue to climb under pressure from inflation.

Mortgage rates keep rising as markets anticipate a near-certain Fed rate hike next month.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage rose to 7.34% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is seven basis points higher than yesterday and 34 basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

On Friday, the Bureau of Labor Statistics released the Producer Price Index for October, which tracks the change in prices that U.S. producers are able to get for their products. This is one way to measure inflation on the market end, rather than the consumer end.

The results were mixed. While prices didn’t rise quite as high as economists anticipated month-over-month (0.3% vs. 0.4%), year-over-year price growth did reach 6.5% — the largest increase in four years. This points to even higher costs for consumers coming in the near future.

Investors are convinced that the Federal Reserve will raise overnight borrowing rates again in December to pull inflation down. And higher overnight borrowing rates — called the federal funds rate, which is what banks pay to fund products like mortgages — usually lead to higher mortgage rates.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.84% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

#3018. Anthropic Priced Its IPO at $83. Here's What I'd Pay for the Stock.

Anthropic's explosive growth has made its IPO one of the biggest events in AI. But its enormous valuation could limit investors' future returns.

By Matt DiLallo – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic, PBC ›

Anthropic's (ANT +0.00%) initial public offering (IPO) is one of the biggest events in the AI industry. The developer of Claude has grown its revenue at an extraordinary pace as businesses adopt its AI tools. The AI giant just priced its IPO at $83 per share, above its expected range of $70 to $78 per share.

However, that price already assumes considerable success. Even if Anthropic becomes one of the world's most successful AI companies, its stock could still deliver disappointing returns from here.

I ran the numbers on what Anthropic's stock could be worth over the next five and 10 years. Here's what they suggest, and the price I'd be willing to pay for the AI stock.

Anthropic recorded $33.2 billion in revenue in just the first nine months of 2026, up a blistering 1,200% compared to the same period last year. Its third-quarter revenue alone was around $17 billion, and its annualized revenue run rate reached $81 billion by Oct. 31, up $10 billion from the prior month.

Enterprise adoption of Claude, its coding tools, API usage, and integrations give Anthropic several avenues for continued growth. However, AI infrastructure and model training are extraordinarily expensive. Anthropic committed to spending a staggering $895.4 billion on compute and infrastructure via purchase commitments and lease agreements. Meanwhile, Anthropic's revenue growth will also almost certainly slow as its business scales.

At $83 per share, Anthropic has a valuation of about $1.5 trillion. That's around 18 times its $81 billion run rate.

That run rate isn't the same as reported annual sales. It extrapolates recent results, which inflates a company growing this quickly. Meanwhile, compute costs, research spending, and future capital needs make it difficult to turn that revenue into sustainable profits. Additionally, stock-based compensation adds dilution, which will be a headwind to per-share earnings growth when the company finally becomes profitable.

The crucial question isn't whether Anthropic is a great company. It's whether its growth can justify a valuation this high.

Using Anthropic competitor, ChatGPT, I modeled three scenarios for Anthropic based on its $83 IPO price. Each one estimates 2031 revenue, net margin, and a price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple, then divides the resulting equity value by a share count that grows 2% a year. Here are my assumptions:

Here's what those assumptions imply for Anthropic's share price:

My base case assumes Anthropic grows its revenue to $400 billion by 2031 with an 18% net margin and a 25x earnings multiple. That would be a remarkable business, yet it would only lift the stock to $91, about 10% above the IPO price, or roughly 2% annually. Meanwhile, my bear case assumes slower growth, thinner margins, and a lower multiple, which would send the shares down roughly 73%. It would take my bull case, with a 24% net margin and a 30x earnings multiple, to deliver a strong return of about 19% annually.

The base case return isn't compelling. Anthropic could execute extremely well and still deliver underwhelming returns because the IPO price already reflects so much success. That's the key takeaway: explosive business growth doesn't guarantee an attractive investment return.

Extending the base case to 2036 improves the outlook, but not by much. Here are the assumptions:

Those assumptions are demanding, as Anthropic would need to double its 2031 revenue and keep widening its margins. Yet the payoff would be only about an 8% annualized return.

I want to earn at least 10% annually from a stock like this using conservative assumptions. For Anthropic's base-case price of $91 in 2031 to deliver that return, I'd need to buy at around $56 per share. That's roughly 30% below the IPO price.

That's why I'd rather wait for a better entry point than chase an elevated first-day price. I'd prefer to buy closer to $55-$60 per share. Even then, I'd want to see that Anthropic can scale its revenue, produce sustainable margins, and defend its position against rival AI platforms.

Anthropic could become one of the most important technology companies of the next decade, and its revenue growth and enterprise adoption are extraordinary. However, the IPO price already reflects that potential, and my base case points to modest returns over the next five years.

I'd rather risk missing the initial excitement than overpay for an exceptional business. The stock gets much more interesting to me at $55 to $60 per share, as long as the underlying growth outlook remains intact.

#3017. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these dividend stocks for boosting portfolio returns

Dividend stocks remain a popular choice for investors seeking steady income and higher portfolio returns. However, with thousands of dividend-paying companies to choose from, identifying the right stocks can be challenging.

In this regard, recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can provide useful insights and help identify dividend stocks backed by solid fundamentals and with attractive upside potential.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR) is this week's first dividend stock. The company recently paid a quarterly base cash dividend of 16 cents per share. At an annualized dividend of 64 cents per share, PR offers a dividend yield of 3.5%.

Recently, Evercore analyst Chris Baker initiated coverage of Permian Resources stock with a price target of $25. The analyst believes that the company is well positioned to benefit from improving U.S. shale demand after the Iran conflict, thanks to its low-breakeven inventory that can boost free cash flow growth. Baker also noted PR's disciplined consolidation in the Permian Basin.

Furthermore, the 5-star analyst highlighted the company's focus on a single basin and management's efficient capital allocations across expansion efforts, strategic acquisitions, and share buybacks. Baker noted that management focuses investments on the higher-return Northern Delaware Basin, helping boost profitability.

"The key piece of our work here, and the reason we think PR deserves a higher multiple relative to more finite or less flexible shale stories, is that PR runs an acquire and exploit model," said Baker.

He explained that Permian Resources deserves a premium valuation as it continually acquires and develops new high-quality assets instead of relying on a limited inventory, a strategy that is the market is underappreciating.

Baker ranks No. 862 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 48.3%. See Permian Resources Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Valero Energy (VLO) is a manufacturer and marketer of petroleum-based and low-carbon liquid transportation fuels and petrochemical products. At a quarterly dividend of $1.20 per share, or annualized dividend of $4.80 per share, VLO stock offers a yield of about 2%.

Heading into Valero's second-quarter earnings on July 30, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on VLO stock and increased the price target to $286 from $283 to reflect updated estimates.

Specifically, the 5-star analyst raised his 2026 and 2027 earnings per share estimates to $31.42 and $23.07 from $29.42 and $21.06, respectively, while leaving the 2028 EPS estimate unchanged at $20.37. Mehta made these revisions based on several factors, including updated commodity price assumptions and changes to refining capture rates.

Despite a strong year-to-date rally in VLO stock, Mehta still finds it compelling due to his more positive refining outlook. Moreover, the analyst sees the possibility of solid estimate revisions, which could drive the stock higher. He believes that Valero is well-positioned to benefit from improving refining market conditions due to its strong position in the Gulf Coast, solid balance sheet strength, and low-cost operations.

"Additionally, we believe the company's premium asset portfolio and crude slate optionality should support capture rates and stronger cash flow generation in the near-term, ultimately supporting shareholder returns," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 742 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.2%. See Valero Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Ovintiv (OVV), a North American oil and natural gas producer. It has solid positions in North America's premier oil basins – the Permian and the Montney. OVV offers a quarterly dividend of 30 cents per share, or an annualized dividend of $1.20 per share, implying a 2.3% yield.

Following meetings with management, RBC Capital analyst Gregory Pardy reaffirmed a buy rating on Ovintiv stock with a price target of $70, highlighting that the stock is on RBC's Global Energy Best Ideas List.

"In our eyes, the depth of Ovintiv's Montney position, streamlined portfolio, strong balance sheet and enhanced shareholder returns afford investors with an attractive valuation re-rating opportunity over time," said Pardy.

The 5-star analyst stated that his meetings with management bolstered his confidence in the company's outlook and potential to achieve a higher valuation. Pardy noted Ovintiv's transformation, with the company streamlining its portfolio from six basins (including the Uinta, Bakken and Anadarko) to two – the Montney and Permian – while enhancing the depth of its inventory.

Pardy also emphasized OVV's improved shareholder returns and solid balance sheet following the recent sale of its assets in the Anadarko Basin for $3 billion.

Pardy ranks No. 169 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 22.3%. See Ovintiv Options Trading Activity on TipRanks.

#3014. Why BellRing Brands Stock Surged Today

People are striving to get more protein in their diets.

By Joe Tenebruso – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:52PM EST

10 stocks we like better than BellRing Brands ›

Shares of BellRing Brands (BRBR +24.07%) jumped 24% on Wednesday after the nutritional supplements maker issued an upbeat earnings forecast for the year ahead.

BellRing's net sales rose 7% year over year to $692 million in its fiscal 2026 fourth quarter, which ended on Sept. 30. The gains were fueled by higher sales of the wellness company's ready-to-drink Premier Protein shakes.

Fitness trends have led more people to seek to increase their protein intake. New offerings, such as Ultimate Protein Shakes that contain 42 grams of protein per bottle, are helping BellRing meet the rising demand for healthier drinks.

Additionally, BellRing recently entered the carbonated beverage market with the launch of its Sparkling Protein Soda, which contains 15 grams of whey protein isolate and only 90 calories and 3 grams of sugar per can.

Distribution gains also contributed to BellRing's sales growth. A new partnership, announced on Nov. 16, will bring BellRing's Ultimate 42g Protein Shakes to 7‑Eleven stores across the U.S.

It should be noted that higher commodity, freight, and tariff-related costs have weighed on BellRing's profits. Yet new CEO Mike Axelrod has a plan to offset these expenses.

"We are advancing a strategic supply chain optimization program targeting at least $100 million in gross savings over the next three years," Axelrod said during a conference call with analysts. "These opportunities span manufacturing, procurement, packaging, and logistics."

BellRing, in turn, sees its earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) rising to between $310 million and $350 million in fiscal 2027, up from $289 million in fiscal 2026.
