#3013. Top Wall Street analysts are confident about these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been under pressure as geopolitical tensions have resurfaced in the Middle East. Moreover, investors remain concerned about the sustainability of AI-driven demand and infrastructure spending.

Nonetheless, those looking for attractive stock picks amid the ongoing volatility can gain key insights by tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. These experts assign ratings after in-depth analysis of a company's fundamentals, growth opportunities, and risks.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

E-commerce and cloud computing giant Amazon (AMZN) is this week's first pick. Heading into the company's second-quarter earnings, TD Cowen analyst John Blackledge reiterated a buy rating on AMZN stock, citing strength in the Amazon Web Services cloud unit as well as the e-commerce and advertising businesses. The analyst lowered his price target on AMZN stock to $340 from $350 as he revised his estimates and slightly raised his capex projections.

Specifically, Blackledge expects Amazon to report revenue of $200.1 billion, 2% above the Street's consensus, driven by acceleration in AWS and advertising revenue. He also expects the company's e-commerce business to reflect the shifting of Prime Day in the U.S. and other key markets to the second quarter this year, compared with third quarter of last year.

In particular, Blackledge expects AWS revenue to grow 35.5% year-over-year in Q2 2026, marking an acceleration from 28.4% in the prior-year quarter and 3.4% above the Street's expectations. The 5-star analyst expects revenue to be driven by rising generative AI workloads as the company's significant AI infrastructure spending helps to ease supply constraints.

Regarding third-quarter outlook, Blackledge said, "Our rev and Op Income estimates are 0.3% and 3.2% above consensus, driven by further AWS revenue growth acceleration led by AI demand."

Blackledge ranks No. 771 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.2%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving on to semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL). Following several meetings with management, RBC Capital analyst Srini Pajjuri reiterated a buy rating on MRVL stock with a price target of $360.

"Overall, the meetings reinforced our conviction that MRVL can sustain 40%+ growth for the next 3 years, driven by strong AI demand, optical connectivity leadership, and expanding Custom pipeline," said Pajjuri.

The 5-star analyst added that robust demand and limited supply are giving greater revenue visibility. Pajjuri noted that Marvell's data center business is on track to deliver more than 50% growth this year and next. Also, the growth in the company's networking business is outpacing compute, driven by agentic AI and inferencing workloads.

Meanwhile, Pajjuri noted that optical product lead times have extended to more than six months, while XPU customers are placing purchase orders 12 months in advance. While the analyst kept his estimates unchanged, he sees the possibility of upside for the second half of 2026 from the Optical business, with more significant upside potential for 2027 and 2028 estimates.

Furthermore, Pajjuri noted that Marvell's scale-across offering is emerging as an additional growth catalyst for 2027, while scale-up networking is expected to present a multibillion-dollar greenfield serviceable addressable market. Also, management is upbeat about Marvell's custom business, with the company targeting more than $10 billion in revenue for 2028, driven by existing programs with Amazon's AWS and Microsoft and multiple XPU attach wins.

Pajjuri ranks No. 88 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 51.5%. See Marvell Options Activity on TipRanks.

Chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices (AMD) is scheduled to announce its second-quarter earnings on Aug. 4. Shares have seen a strong jump year-to-date due to demand for the company's AI GPUs and server CPUs.

Ahead of second-quarter earnings, Wells Fargo analyst Aaron Rakers reaffirmed a buy rating on AMD stock and raised his price target to $615 from $505, citing "increasing focus on path to +$20/sh. EPS in CY28." The analyst expects AMD to reiterate its confidence in the MI450 series and Helios ramp beginning in the third quarter of 2026.

The 5-star analyst increased his estimates for AMD server CPU revenue to $16.0 billion (up 68% year-over-year), $20.5 billion (up 28%), and $25.0 billion (up 22%) for 2026, 2027, 2028, respectively. Rakers noted that in the previous quarter, AMD increased its server CPU total addressable market estimates to $120 billion by 2030, representing a more than 35% compound annual growth rate.

Rakers expects AMD to comment on an additional rise in server CPU demand since its first-quarter results. He sees upside driven by agentic AI demand momentum, cloud demand, and traditional enterprise modernization. In this regard, the analyst highlighted that Micron increased its 2026 server shipment guidance recently. Also, checks indicate continued upside to the average selling price.

Meanwhile, Rakers' data center GPU estimates remain above consensus at $15.6 billion, $40.6 billion, and $63.0 billion for 2026, 2027, and 2028, respectively, while estimates for the client and gaming businesses are below the Street's consensus. Overall, the analyst projects EPS of $7.15, $13.40, and $18.75 for 2026, 2027, and 2028, respectively.

#3012. Chase Adds Qantas, Cathay Pacific as Transfer Partners

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The new additions bring Chase’s total number of transfer partners to 16.

Chase is growing its roster of airline transfer partners to include Qantas Frequent Flyer and Cathay Pacific Asia Miles.

That’ll bump the number of Chase’s transfer partners to 16, with 12 airline and four hotel loyalty programs. The new partners, which are live as of Nov. 18, 2026, will transfer at a 1:1 ratio from Chase Ultimate Rewards®.

You’ll need an eligible Chase card, such as the Chase Sapphire Reserve® and the Chase Sapphire Preferred® Card, to transfer points.

Qantas and Cathay Pacific are the flag carriers for Australia and Hong Kong, respectively.

Both airlines are part of the Oneworld alliance, which includes U.S. carriers like American Airlines, Alaska Airlines and Hawaiian Airlines. They also maintain relationships with non-alliance partners.

Your best shot at award seats on Qantas and Cathay Pacific flights is often through their own programs. That’s because airlines typically release the most award availability to their own loyalty program members.

While some partner airline programs might offer cheaper award pricing, it can be harder to find award availability through them. That’s why having points and miles with the airline you want to fly on can be so valuable.

This is especially helpful for airlines that operate highly sought-after routes or premium cabin products. For example, Cathay Pacific's Aria Suite in business class is a popular choice for flyers. It's available on select flights from Hong Kong to North America, Europe, Australia and parts of Asia, so check the seat map before booking. For example, Cathay Pacific’s Aria Suite in business class is a popular choice for flyers and is only available on select routes.

Meanwhile, Qantas is expected to start flying the world's longest commercial route in October 2027 between Sydney and London, as part of its Project Sunrise initiative. It’s also slated to launch nonstop flights between Sydney and New York the following year.

Those seats aren’t on sale yet, but they’re likely to be highly coveted for award travelers.

These additions aren’t particularly exciting if you already have access to other transferable currencies like Capital One miles, which transfer 1:1 to both Qantas and Cathay Pacific.

However, if you’re all in on Chase points, these new airline partners can unlock more award redemption opportunities.

The best way to earn a large tranche of Chase points is through a welcome offer, available to new applicants on select cards. If you’re looking to add a card that earns Chase points to your wallet, here are some options:

• 5x points on all Chase Travel℠ purchases (exclusions apply).

• 5x on Lyft rides and Peloton equipment (for a limited time; terms apply).

• 3x points on dining, takeout and eligible delivery worldwide.

• 3x points for online grocery purchases (exclusions apply).

• 3x points for vacation homes booked through platforms like Airbnb and Vrbo.

• 8 points per $1 spent on all travel booked through Chase.

• 4 points per $1 spent on bookings directly through an airline or hotel.

• 3 points per $1 spent on dining, takeout and eligible delivery worldwide.

• 1 point per $1 spent on all other purchases.

#3009. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The ongoing earnings season, investor concerns about the durability of AI demand and spending, and geopolitical risks are key factors that have been contributing to stock market volatility in recent trading sessions.

In this scenario, investors seeking steady income can consider adding dividend stocks to their portfolios. Recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help them pick attractive dividend stocks that are backed by solid cash flows to support consistent payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips is this week's first dividend pick. With a dividend of 84 cents per share (annualized dividend of $3.36 per share), COP offers a dividend yield of 3%. The company is scheduled to announce its second-quarter results on Aug. 6.

Ahead of second-quarter results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin reiterated a buy rating on COP stock with a price target of $183. Despite the pressure on oil prices from an increase in OPEC production quota, the analyst finds ConocoPhillips and Shell stocks appealing as the earnings season approaches. He cited their operational visibility and resilience as factors backing their appeal.

The 5-star analyst expects ConocoPhillips to meet its production guidance of 2.2 million barrels of oil equivalent per day at the mid-point. He expects lower Waha natural gas prices in the Permian Basin to be offset by stronger Brent crude premiums. Margolin expects capital expenditure to remain within COP's prior guided range of $12.2 billion annualized, with no significant impact on spending on the Northfield East project in Qatar despite the Strait of Hormuz disruption.

Overall, Margolin expects COP to generate about $3.5 billion in free cash flow (before working capital) and earnings per share of $2.94. He expects continued strength in COP's free cash flow and regular dividend growth through the completion of the Willow project in 2028/2029. Prior to the Willow project coming online, the analyst expects free cash flow to grow by about $2 billion in 2027 and 2028, assuming Brent crude averages around $60 per barrel.

"COP's track record of capital efficiency and strong Permian well productivity underpins its ability to pursue long-cycle developments," said Margolin.

Margolin ranks No. 457 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 70% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.3%. See ConocoPhillips Financials on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer is a limited partnership that operates 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. With a quarterly cash distribution of 33.75 cents per common unit ($1.35 per unit on an annualized basis), ET offers a yield of 6.8%.

Heading into Energy Transfer's Q2 earnings on Aug. 4, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith reaffirmed a buy rating on ET stock with a price target of $23. The analyst noted that his adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization estimate of $4.46 billion is 1% below the Street's consensus of $4.49 billion.

The 5-star analyst noted that Energy Transfer has slightly outperformed Enterprise Products Partners recently. However, it still trades at a relative discount of 19% compared to EPD, which is below its historical discount range of 17%-20%. Smith believes that ET stock could be re-rated higher if the company provides a clearer long-term strategy for natural gas growth.

Furthermore, Smith expects the current energy market to support a stronger outlook for natural gas liquids and crude oil. "The current energy macro backdrop positions ET to benefit in all three commodities," said the analyst.

He expects Energy Transfer's adjusted EBITDA to grow at a 4.8% compound annual growth rate in 2027-2030, which is 1%-3% above Wall Street's expectations. In fact, Smith sees the possibility of additional upside if ET announces more natural gas projects. He added that investors will await details on final investment decisions on new natural gas projects and any clues about additional projects in the pipeline. The analyst noted that ET has announced new gas projects consistently in recent quarters.

Smith ranks No. 550 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.4%. See Energy Transfer Statistics on TipRanks.

Finally, let's look at energy giant Chevron, which is scheduled to announce its second-quarter results on July 31. Last month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12, CVX offers a dividend yield of 3.92%.

Ahead of Q2 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on Chevron stock and lowered his price target to $216 from $236. Byrne expects the company to report adjusted EPS of about $5.86 per share, nearly 9% above the Street's expectations.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the challenges seen in Chevron's upstream business in the first quarter due to the disruption at the Tengizchevroil joint venture in Kazakhstan, Storm Fern downtime, and the Middle East conflict have largely been resolved. Consequently, Byrne expects production to recover in the second quarter to about 4,033 mboepd. He expects the upstream business to generate adjusted earnings of about $8.1 billion in Q2 2026.

Meanwhile, Byrne expects Chevron to generate downstream adjusted earnings of about $4.4 billion in Q2, with strength in both domestic and international markets. The downstream business benefited from higher crack spreads and strong refining performance.

Additionally, the analyst expects Chevron to generate $18.2 billion in cash flow from operations (before working capital changes), driven by stronger earnings and about $2.2 billion in dividends from affiliated companies. Unlike the first quarter, Chevron is not expected to make a TCO loan repayment in Q2, providing an additional boost to cash flow.

Byrne ranks No. 409 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 56% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.5%. See Chevron Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#3007. Is Anthropic’s IPO a Good Reason to Buy Ginkgo Bioworks Right Now?

The AI developer's offering will give it a pile of cash that it might choose to spend on biomedical research automation.

By Alex Carchidi – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:56PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Ginkgo Bioworks ›

Anthropic (ANT +0.00%) is planning to move forward with its initial public offering (IPO), with trading expected to begin Nov. 19. In doing so, the AI company will raise tens of billions of dollars, which it plans to spend on computing power and infrastructure. But with its focus increasingly turning toward orienting its AI capabilities toward bioscience, the next few years will likely see it make major investments to secure the ingredients it needs to test the hypotheses its AI proposes.

Chief among those ingredients are lab space and biomedical research equipment -- and, as it's a tech company bent on automation, laboratory robotics and other research automation solutions will be near the top of the list.

Enter Ginkgo Bioworks (DNA +1.44%), a lab-automation biotech and aspiring biomanufacturer that's already collaborating with Anthropic's biggest competitor, OpenAI. While many other businesses offer different components of an automated biopharma research stack, Ginkgo says its cart-based system is more modular, expandable, robust, and customizable than any competing platform on the market. So does Anthropic, and its soon-to-land massive pile of money make Ginkgo worth buying right now?

Ginkgo has a unique approach to developing a fully autonomous lab designed to handle multiple types of experiments with minimal hands-on time.

Its solution features a series of robotic carts, each with a central cabinet with an optional small sterile environment. They are connected so they can pass samples and data along after each experiment, while the software plans the next experiments. Humans set the system's goals and check its results. Each cart typically has at least one piece of laboratory hardware in its cabinet, such as a liquid-handling robot, a centrifuge, or an analyzer. Ginkgo's Nebula lab runs more than 100 of these reconfigurable carts.

OpenAI has already put one of its advanced models in charge of a Ginkgo lab. In OpenAI's pilot program, GPT-5 took on the role of AI scientist, designing 36,000 small experiments over a period of six months that Ginkgo's cart-based robots then ran while humans prepped the necessary reagents. Ultimately, the pilot program supposedly reduced the production cost of a test protein by 40%.

In third-quarter 2026, Ginkgo said frontier AI labs also use its cloud lab (lab work customers order remotely) for getting more training data, and it even sold its first autonomous lab to Novo Nordisk (NVO -3.12%). Still, its Q3 revenue fell 6% year over year to $28 million, and it reported a $41 million net loss from continuing operations. One important thing to remember here is that a large portion of early biopharma research is done manually, which means paying labor costs rather than capital expenditures for automation hardware.

Anthropic already has a bioscience laboratory, and it contracts out some of its physical scientific workload when it's efficient to do so. For example, a muscular dystrophy program it recently acquired went on to coordinate experiments across 11 external labs. That's a very high number of collaborators to juggle for a project in preclinical-stage research. Ginkgo could be competing to be one of those collaborators in the future, as contract research organizations (CROs) doubtlessly were and are.

However, Anthropic has no public ties to Ginkgo, unlike OpenAI. Its Model Hardware Standard, which is intended to enable AI agents to operate lab equipment, has eight vendor partners, including a lab-automation business called Automata, but not Ginkgo. Since that system is intended to work with any programmable device, it means rivals’ lab automation hardware would work just as easily as Ginkgo’s, making its capabilities a harder sell.

Anyone wanting upside from Anthropic's biology push can buy its stock once it's listed. It's very possible that Ginkgo will work with Anthropic in the future. As of now, though, the upcoming IPO isn't something to bank on for Ginkgo’s stock, and it doesn’t constitute a distinct reason to buy it yet.

#3003. e.l.f. Beauty vs. On: Which Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

One of these brands is growing sales at roughly 40% while the other is trading at a discount to the market.

By John Ballard – Updated Nov 18, 2026 at 9:04PM EST

10 stocks we like better than e.l.f. Beauty ›

Investors often look for disruptive growth stories when evaluating the consumer space. Today we compare e.l.f. Beauty (ELF +2.30%) and On (ONON +1.82%) to determine which stock offers a more compelling opportunity for long-term growth.

e.l.f. Beauty has built a loyal following by offering vegan and cruelty-free cosmetics at accessible prices. Meanwhile, On is a Swiss athletic brand gaining significant market share from legacy footwear giants. Both companies are successfully leveraging digital engagement and strategic retail partnerships to outpace their respective industries.

e.l.f. Beauty operates as a multi-brand beauty company, offering cosmetics and skincare through brands like e.l.f. SKIN and Naturium. It primarily serves value-conscious consumers through major retail stores including Target, Walmart, and Amazon.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached $1.9 billion, representing growth of approximately 40% compared to the prior period. Net income was roughly $87 million over the trailing four quarters.

As of its Sept. 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is 2.6x, which measures a company's ability to cover short-term liabilities with short-term assets. The debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.8x, a metric that compares total debt to shareholder equity to assess financial leverage.

Trailing-12-month free cash flow was $276 million. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 41% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation, since SBC is a non-cash expense added back to cash flow.

On is a Swiss premium sportswear brand that designs and sells footwear, apparel, and accessories globally. The company focuses on technical innovation and a premium brand image to attract runners and lifestyle consumers across the Americas, Europe, and Asia. It utilizes a mix of direct-to-consumer sales and selective wholesale partnerships to maintain high brand equity and control its pricing strategy.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached $4 billion, an increase of approximately 11% year over year. Net income for the period was nearly $527 million, reflecting a healthy net profit margin of 13%. The company has demonstrated a strong ability to scale its premium products while maintaining healthy bottom-line results.

As of its Sept. 2026 balance sheet, On carries a debt-to-equity ratio of roughly 0.4x. Its current ratio is approximately 3.1x, indicating a solid buffer of liquid assets to meet its upcoming financial obligations. Trailing free cash flow reached $478 million, providing the company with ample capital to reinvest in its global retail expansion.

e.l.f. Beauty must navigate a highly competitive landscape filled with both massive multinational corporations and nimble independent brands. The company relies heavily on a limited number of major retail partners, meaning the loss of shelf space at Target or Walmart could materially impact its results. Furthermore, its dependence on third-party manufacturers in China exposes the business to potential supply chain disruptions, shifts in international trade policy, or new tariffs. The integration of recent acquisitions also presents a risk if expected synergies fail to materialize.

On Holding faces intense competition from established global footwear players with significantly larger marketing budgets and distribution networks. While the brand is currently in high demand, it must continually innovate to keep pace with evolving fashion trends and athletic performance requirements. Managing inventory levels across diverse global regions is another constant challenge that could impact profitability if demand shifts unexpectedly. Additionally, as a premium brand, its sales remain sensitive to broader economic cycles that might limit discretionary spending on luxury sportswear.

On appears more attractive from a valuation perspective, as it trades at a lower multiple of both its future earnings estimates and its sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

These brands are both performing at a high level in a choppy consumer spending environment. I would buy e.l.f. Beauty, mainly because it is seeing accelerating growth and improving margins. I like On, too, which trades at a lower P/E multiple, but e.l.f. is growing revenue significantly faster through a challenging stretch for consumer brands. I think e.l.f. is building a powerful "house of brands" portfolio that is resonating at home and abroad.

E.l.f. posted a 51% year-over-year increase in net sales last quarter, marking the 31st straight quarter of 20% or better growth. Rhode seems to be getting more popular with each new release, while the company's Naturium brand is one of the fastest-growing in the skincare space.

E.l.f. has become the top cosmetics brand on Amazon, and management says it's also the most searched brand on the platform. People are seeking out the brand, which means the company doesn't have to spend as much on marketing to acquire new customers. This supports profitable expansion.

On is an attractive buy right now, too, as it continues to post double-digit growth while the stock trades at a modest forward P/E of about 18 -- a slight discount to the S&P 500's 19x forward P/E. But I prefer investing in reasonably priced growth stocks of companies that are growing revenue at higher rates. At the rate e.l.f. continues to grow with ample room to expand internationally, I believe the stock has a favorable chance of outperforming in 2027.

#3002. Top Wall Street analysts back these 3 stocks for their long-term growth potential

Major indexes remain volatile as investors assess key earnings releases and geopolitical tensions in the Middle East. Investors with a long-term horizon should look beyond the ongoing noise and focus on stocks that are well-positioned to generate strong returns.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist stocks with robust growth opportunities. These experts conduct an in-depth analysis of all the macro and micro factors affecting a company, focusing on its long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

CrowdStrike Holdings is a cloud-native cybersecurity provider. The company recently announced an expanded partnership with Schwarz Digits to bring its Falcon platform to European enterprises.

After hosting several European investor meetings with the CrowdStrike CFO, Stifel analyst Adam Borg reiterated a buy rating on CRWD stock and increased his price target to $230 from $220.

The five-star analyst said that the meetings increased his confidence about CrowdStrike being an "important, well-positioned cybersecurity platform vendor that is an AI beneficiary given its expansive portfolio, unique data set, and ability to both secure AI and use AI to improve security."

Among the key takeaways, Borg noted that AI is driving a major shift in cybersecurity demand as it presents software vulnerabilities that can be exploited by less sophisticated hackers with capabilities that were once limited to government-backed hacking groups. This has led to companies making cybersecurity a top priority, driving customer interest in CrowdStrike's new AI Detection & Response solution and its broader security platform.

Borg added that AI-driven demand is expanding CrowdStrike's pipeline and supporting confidence in the company's higher fiscal 2027 net new annual recurring revenue guidance issued last quarter. Overall, Borg believes that CrowdStrike has multiple growth drivers that can help maintain revenue growth in at least the high-teens and drive improved profitability in the years ahead.

Borg ranks No. 651 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See CrowdStrike Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving on to AST SpaceMobile, a company building a global cellular broadband network in space. Recently, Piper Sandler analyst Alexander Potter initiated coverage of space-related stocks. While the analyst assigned AST SpaceMobile a buy rating with a price target of $100, he has a neutral stance on rocket builders SpaceX and Rocket Lab due to valuation concerns.

"We prefer ASTS, due to a more palatable valuation, and a clearer path to EBITDA upside," said Potter.

The five-star analyst highlighted that AST SpaceMobile builds satellites that connect directly to smartphones, ensuring network connectivity for users everywhere and enabling applications like video calls, streaming, and gaming. Potter added that ASTS' technology helps Mobile Network Operators, or MNOs, expand coverage and offer add-on services to travelers and users in remote locations.

Potter ranks No. 708 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 48% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.2%. See AST SpaceMobile Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Finally, let's look at Broadcom, a provider of custom AI chips and networking solutions. Recently, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on Broadcom stock with a price target of $502.

The five-star analyst is surprised by the underperformance of AVGO stock so far this year compared to its peers, especially given continued momentum in AI-driven demand. Moore cited concerns that competitor MediaTek could gain market share from Broadcom in Google's tensor processing unit chip business and investor preference for faster-growing AI chip names as reasons for the underperformance.

While Moore acknowledged that MediaTek's role in Google's TPU business is growing, he contends that it is unlikely to disrupt Broadcom's position significantly. He expects Broadcom to continue to be the major TPU supplier, with about 80% market share. Overall, Moore believes that concerns about a decline in AVGO's market share to 50% or its eventual replacement seem premature.

"AVGO should retain majority share, sustain strong AI growth, and remain a core compute winner, close behind NVDA," said Moore.

The analyst explained that his bullish outlook on Broadcom is based on the company's solid advantage in high-bandwidth memory supply, chip packaging capabilities, and large-scale production. Moore also highlighted AVGO's dominance in AI ASIC chips, strong networking business, and new customer wins.

Moore ranks No. 148 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 25.5%. See Broadcom Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

#2999. World of Hyatt Adds Milestone Rewards, New Delta Benefits

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Hyatt is updating its rewards program in 2027, and it means you'll earn Milestone Rewards even sooner than before.

Editor/Content Strategist & Smart Travel Podcast Host

It’s going to be easier to rack up extra benefits if you’re a frequent Hyatt customer in 2027. World of Hyatt announced new Milestone Rewards, the ability to stack awards (such as Free Night Certificates and Suite Upgrade Awards) on a single stay and a simpler 365-day expiration on all awards.

The news expands on the fresh details revealed Nov. 17, 2026, around World of Hyatt’s partnership with Delta SkyMiles, which was first announced in September.

The additions to the Milestone Rewards are designed to give members more choice and personalization, according to Laurie Blair, senior vice president of global marketing and loyalty at Hyatt.

"We’re shaping the future of World of Hyatt around what our members value most. After a year of listening, one thing is clear: members want more distinctive benefits, more freedom to use their awards and the opportunity to choose awards that matter most to them,” Blair said in an email to NerdWallet.

But top-tier Globalist members will soon have to choose between perks that they previously received automatically. Most notably, Globalists will no longer receive free parking on award nights in 2027. Instead, they can choose a Free Parking Award as a Milestone Reward that they can apply to award or paid stays.

The Delta Air Lines and Hyatt partnership will also mainly benefit frequent flyers and hotel guests. With the exception of the limited-time intro promotion, only Delta or Hyatt members with elite status will earn points in both programs. Hyatt elites will have to purchase premium tickets on Delta — not economy — to earn Hyatt points on the flight.

Starting Jan. 1, 2027, World of Hyatt will extend Milestone Rewards to guests who have not yet qualified for the first level of elite status. Southwest Airlines did the same in its recent revamp to give loyal customers a thank you before they’ve reached any level of elite status.

World of Hyatt members will reach the first Milestone Reward at just 10 nights or 20,000 Base Points. Previously, it was 20 nights or 35,000 Base Points.

The reward choices at the first milestone are:

A tier-qualifying night credit, which gets you closer to the next level of elite status.

A $25 FIND Experience credit (Hyatt’s platform for local experiences such as cooking classes).

A $30 spa credit good at Hyatt Inclusive Collection resorts.

The latter two will likely still require you to pay money out of pocket to use them, as the credit will only cover a portion of your bill. For example, the cheapest spa treatment at Secrets Playa Mujeres Golf & Spa Resort in Cancun, Mexico, will still run you $109 (or $79 after the credit).

The Milestone Rewards start to get good at the higher tiers. Starting at 20 nights, you can select from the existing Milestone Rewards, as well as new items. Some of the new options include:

Free parking on award nights is going away as a Globalist tier benefit. In 2027, a Free Parking Award will be an option that Globalists can select at the 60, 70, 80 and 90 nights The parking award can be used on either a qualifying award or paid stay for up to seven nights.

Hyatt said in a call with NerdWallet that roughly 70% of Globalists weren’t using the free parking benefit at all.

Globalists who have earned status in 2026 for 2027 will be grandfathered into the automatic free parking on award stays through their tier expiration date in February 2028.

To use these new awards, the Hyatt booking process is also getting an upgrade. Previously, awards like Suite Upgrade Awards and Free Night Awards couldn’t be redeemed on the same stay. Starting in 2027, members can combine eligible awards on a single reservation, including Suite Upgrade Awards, Guest of Honor Awards and Free Parking Awards.

The new booking system will also make it easier to pay for some nights of a single reservation with awards, points, cash or a combination of points and cash.

Every award earned going forward will also carry a 365-day expiration from the date it’s issued, replacing the patchwork of different validity windows. Previously, Suite Upgrade and Guest of Honor Awards expired at “rest of year plus 14 days,” which was just a tad confusing. Meanwhile, Free Night Awards earned through Milestone Rewards were valid for 180 days from the date of issuance, which was not a lot of time to plan a trip.

The Milestone Rewards kick in next year, but the other big change around the Delta partnership is available now.

Hyatt elite members can earn World of Hyatt Bonus Points on eligible Delta flights (Delta Comfort, Premium Select, First Class and Delta One). Basic Economy or standard Main Cabin fares will earn nothing from this partnership at all.

Discoverist members earn 1 Bonus Point per $2 spent.

Explorist, Globalist and Lifetime Globalist members earn 1 Bonus Point per $1 spent.

Delta Medallion members (Silver through Diamond) can earn 1 Delta mile per $1 spent at participating Hyatt properties, on top of their regular Hyatt points.

#2997. SpaceX’s December Lock-Up Frees Another 17% of Its Shares. Here’s What October’s Unlock Did to the Stock.

SpaceX's public float continues to balloon.

By Ryan Vanzo – Nov 18, 2026 at 9:35PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

Space Exploration Technologies' (SPCX +1.27%) initial public offering this summer was unique in several ways.

It was one of the biggest IPOs in history. It was also one of the largest public debuts ever for an unprofitable business. Finally, the company went public with less than 5% of its shares available for public purchase -- far below the industry average.

There are some benefits to going public with only a small fraction of your total share count available for trade. First, it limits initial selling pressure, as most of the shares are locked until future milestones are met. Second, it can stack the odds in favor of a successful debut, as buying pressure can easily overwhelm selling pressure.

There are downsides to SpaceX's approach, however. Over time, more SpaceX shares become available for trading, adding potential selling pressure. Some events call for large share unlocks, potentially flooding the market with tradable shares over a short period.

Several analysts were worried about a potential overhang from major share unlock events. Morningstar (MORN -0.65%) analysts, for example, were concerned not only with SpaceX's initial IPO valuation, but also the company's unusual staggered lockup schedule.

"We think long-term investors eager to participate in SpaceX's future endeavors and potential success will have opportunities to do so with more margin of safety than the initial offering is likely to provide," the firm concluded. SpaceX's lockup schedule, one Morningstar analyst warned, could create "regular bouts of selling pressure."

Next month, one of SpaceX's biggest share-unlock events will take place, freeing up roughly 17% of its total locked shares for sale. Will this create selling pressure as some experts predict?

The best way to answer this question is by analyzing SpaceX's October share price action. That month, several unlock events sent the company's public float soaring. The impact is critical for SpaceX investors to understand.

SpaceX experienced three share unlock events in October.

The first was on Oct. 9 when 328.4 million shares became eligible for trading. That represented around 7% of the shares covered by the lockup schedule, excluding founder shares, mostly owned by Elon Musk, which won't be eligible for sale until next summer. Shares barely responded to this even, moving higher by 1% on the day.

The next event on Oct. 22 triggered the release of 1.3 billion shares. That's around 28% of the locked-up pool. That trading session was rough, with SpaceX stock falling roughly 9%.

The final October unlock event began Oct. 24 with the release of 328.4 million shares valued at nearly $50 billion. Shares fell by just 0.4% the next day of trading.

First, major unlock events can affect a company's near-term stock price. SpaceX's smaller unlocks had little immediate effect. But its larger unlock events seemingly sent shares plunging. Despite strong third-quarter earnings and 11 successful rocket launches, shares fell roughly 9% in October.

Second, share unlock events can create buying opportunities. SpaceX's intrinsic value as a business does not change as more shares are made available for trade. The share price action is simply a reflection of short-term supply and demand dynamics. If you like SpaceX as a company but have been waiting for a better entry point, the next share unlock event on Dec. 9 could be your chance to jump in.

With a market cap of nearly $2 trillion, I'm still on the sidelines when it comes to SpaceX stock. As I explained recently, "SpaceX's current valuation is tough to justify based on launch services and Starlink potential alone. The company must execute on its AI roadmap to justify a valuation above $1 trillion."

SpaceX's AI roadmap, however, will be difficult to fully achieve. It relies on several high-risk, high-reward efforts, including the successful commercialization of Starship -- which would become the largest rocket to ever reach orbit -- and the successful deployments of orbital data centers, which some experts believe will never be economical.

December's unlock event could create additional selling pressure, providing investors with a more attractive entry point. But it still might not be enough for me to jump in.

#2993. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 dividend stocks for passive income

The uncertainty in the Middle East and concerns about the durability of the artificial intelligence boom and high spending continue to weigh on investor sentiment. Investors can consider strengthening their portfolios by adding dividend stocks.

Tracking top Wall Street analysts can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts provide useful insights after thorough analysis of a company's financials and growth prospects.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first dividend pick is natural gas producer Expand Energy, which recently announced the $1.25 billion acquisition of Twin Eagle Holdings, a private asset-backed natural gas marketing and optimization business. The deal is expected to accelerate EXE's marketing and commercial goals.

Meanwhile, Expand Energy impressed investors with solid second-quarter results. It repurchased shares worth $530 million in Q2 2026 and announced an additional $1 billion of buyback authorization. Expand declared a dividend of nearly 58 cents per share, payable on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend per share of $2.30, EXE stock offers a dividend yield of 2.5%.

Following the Q2 print, Wolfe Research analyst Doug Leggate reiterated a buy rating on EXE stock and raised his price target to $114 from $110. The five-star analyst noted that Expand delivered market-beating adjusted earnings per share and earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization, driven by better gas realizations and lower gathering, processing, and transportation expenses.

"Solid 2Q26 with in-line guidance keeps our focus on what it can control, walking the line between options to return value to s/holders," said Leggate.

As Expand Energy continues to weigh options to enhance shareholder returns, Leggate believes that net debt reduction is the best choice. He highlighted that the company repaid $1.3 billion of gross debt in April, supported by seasonally higher cash flows in the first quarter. EXE ended Q2 2026 with $3.1 billion in net debt. Leggate views buybacks as the second-best option and mergers and acquisitions as the third, with the Twin Eagle deal expected to lower Expand's breakeven by about 7 cents from about $2.80/Mcf.

Leggate ranks No. 807 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.1%. See Expand Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to SM Energy, an operator of oil and gas assets across four leading U.S. shale basins: the Permian Basin, DJ Basin, South Texas, and Uinta Basin. SM offers a quarterly dividend of 22 cents per share, or an annualized dividend of 88 cents per share, representing a yield of about 2.7%.

Following a preliminary update on Q2 metrics, Roth analyst Leo Mariani reaffirmed a buy rating on SM Energy stock and raised his price target to $34 from $32. The analyst cited higher oil prices and his optimism about a strong second-quarter report as the reasons for the upgraded price target.

Commenting on the key insights from the Q2 preliminary update, Mariani noted strong oil and gas price realizations in the quarter. However, the update reflected a slightly larger cash hedging loss of $220 million from derivatives, compared to Roth's estimate of about $211 million.

Meanwhile, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow per share estimate by 3%, citing a strong Q2 2026 pricing update. Mariani added that he expects SM Energy to report strong Q2 2026 results on Aug. 5, with oil production of 237,650 barrels of oil per day, which is around 1.5% above the Street's estimate. He expects capital expenditure of $820 million, in line with consensus.

"We rate SM Energy a Buy based on its reasonable returns of capital to shareholders, its discounted valuation vs. peers, and its upside potential in the Austin Chalk and Uinta plays," said Mariani.

Mariani ranks No. 65 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 27%. See SM Energy Financials on TipRanks.

Oilfield services provider SLB, formerly known as Schlumberger, reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings. The company attributed its Q2 performance to sequential growth across international markets, mainly led by offshore activity in Latin America, Europe and Africa, as well as Asia, helping offset disruptions in the Middle East due to the U.S.-Iran conflict.

Within Goldman's oilfield services coverage, Mehta is optimistic about SLB as he expects the company to benefit from a recovery in international activity. The five-star analyst projects international revenue growth of about 10% between 2026 and 2027, driven by SLB's exposure to incremental intervention activity and a recovery in Middle East activity. He also expects international revenue to gain from higher offshore activity and a potential ramp-up of exploration activity across several geographies.

Furthermore, Mehta's bullish stance is backed by long-term growth prospects of SLB's data center business. He noted that the company expects the annual run-rate revenue of this business to exceed $2 billion by the end of 2027, with the potential to grow the customer base and products. Mehta also expects SLB to deliver strong free cash flow and shareholder returns this year, driven by higher activity and unique business lines.

#2992. La-Z-Boy: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Its U.S.-made upholstery and consolidated distribution hubs give investors a supply chain built to weather trade policy swings.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 18, 2026 at 10:09PM EST

On its fiscal 2027 second-quarter earnings call, held on Nov. 18, 2026, La-Z-Boy (LZB +3.63%) reported a 4% increase in consolidated delivered sales, excluding the impact of its wholesale casegoods divestiture. The company relies on retail store expansion and supply chain consolidation to strengthen its competitive position in a choppy market. Three key takeaways from management's comments stand out.

La-Z-Boy added three company-owned stores during the second quarter. Corporate ownership now represents 62% of its 380-store network. Owning more retail locations gives the company direct control over pricing and customer experience while capturing higher gross margins.

Management is consolidating Joybird production into its U.S. manufacturing network and reducing regional distribution centers from 15 facilities to three central hubs. Producing products domestically shields the business from ocean freight disruptions and shifting tariff policies.

The company returned $32 million to shareholders through dividends and share repurchases in the second quarter. Management maintains a clear framework to reinvest half of operating cash flow into the business while returning the rest to investors.

Choppy market conditions test furniture demand, but domestic manufacturing and network expansion preserve market share. Management monitors holiday traffic while maintaining pricing discipline.

Zero external debt provides financial flexibility to complete distribution upgrades while returning steady capital to shareholders, positioning the company for long-term growth.

#2990. Mortgage Rates Today, Wednesday, November 18: Moving Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many homebuyers.

Mortgage rates are lower today, but recent averages above 7% are still eating into buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.13% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 21 basis points lower than yesterday and 13 basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

On Friday, the Bureau of Labor Statistics released the Producer Price Index for October, which tracks the change in prices that U.S. producers are able to get for their products. This is one way to measure inflation on the market end, rather than the consumer end.

The results were mixed. While prices didn’t rise quite as high as economists anticipated month-over-month (0.3% vs. 0.4%), year-over-year price growth did reach 6.5% — the largest increase in four years. This points to even higher costs for consumers coming in the near future.

Investors are convinced that the Federal Reserve will raise overnight borrowing rates again in December to pull inflation down. And higher overnight borrowing rates — called the federal funds rate, which is what banks pay to fund products like mortgages — usually lead to higher mortgage rates.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.63% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#2986. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh Vowed to Hit the Fed's 2% Inflation Target After October's Rate Hold, Even as JPMorgan Warns Democratic Control of the Senate Could Make Him a One-Term Chair. Here's Why That Political Risk Matters for Markets.

Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh is focusing on process, but that may not matter if he ignores this part of his job.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 18, 2026 at 10:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than JPMorgan Chase ›

JPMorgan Chase (JPM -1.77%) recently published a report that went well beyond Wall Street analysis. In it, chief U.S. economist Michael Feroli warned that current Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh needs to broaden his focus, or all the changes he is making could prove for naught. It isn't an idle warning, and investors need to understand why politics matters for a position intended to be apolitical. The key to the story is that Kevin Warsh risks serving a one-term tenure as chair if he doesn't engage more with Congress. Here's why it matters.

From a big-picture perspective, the Fed's job is to ensure stable prices and full employment. That dual mandate can be difficult to manage because elevated inflation often accompanies a strong economy. It is a delicate balancing act meant to occur outside the political process. This is why the Fed is self-funded, board members serve long terms, and they can be removed only for cause. This setup is meant to curb the short-term thinking that often accompanies the political process, in which elections are held every two years.

Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.

That's not to suggest that the Fed can completely ignore politicians. The president appoints key members of the Fed to their positions, with the Senate's approval. The Fed also has to make regular updates to Congress. So while the job of Federal Reserve Chair is meant to be outside of the political process, it is still entwined with politics. That's the core of the warning that JPMorgan Chase's Michael Feroli is making.

According to Feroli, current Fed Chair Kevin Warsh is making a mistake by not engaging more with Congress. And given the shifting political winds in Washington, he could be replaced when his term ends in 2030 if he doesn't change what he is doing in this area. This warning is likely tied to the fact that Warsh is making major changes in other areas of his job.

Even as the Fed has made strong statements about returning inflation to its 2% target, Kevin Warsh has been vocal about reducing the amount of guidance it provides to the market. Wall Street got used to receiving material guidance after the dot-com crash and the Great Recession. Effectively, the Fed was looking to ease investor concerns during deep bear markets and economic downturns. However, the guidance didn't end after those difficult periods.

Warsh believes this has left the market reliant on the Fed rather than operating as it should, setting prices in real time based on economic conditions. As such, he's started to limit the amount of information the Fed shares. Wall Street isn't pleased, particularly with inflation elevated and rates on the rise. The bond market has been particularly volatile during this transition. For those who have been around long enough, however, this is just a return to more normal times, even though it is not an easy transition.

That is where Warsh's interactions with Congress come in. If he is only a one-term Fed chair, the changes could quickly be rolled back by the next chairman. If he takes the time to better explain what he is doing and why, he would increase the odds of being appointed to a second term. That would help the changes he has made stick, which, in turn, could give the Fed more leeway to address its dual mandate over the long term.

Warsh is focused on fixing "the process," which is laudable. There is a moral hazard in Wall Street expecting the Fed to provide excessive information and, in some quarters, to serve as a backstop for the market. But fixing the process isn't happening in a vacuum, and Warsh would increase the odds of success if he took the time to more fully, and perhaps personally, explain to Congress what he is doing and why.

While that sounds political, and it is to some extent, it may be more of a social issue. If Warsh doesn't build trust and respect, he risks reducing the support he'll receive when his term ends. The risk for investors is that the Fed's approach could swing between extremes, which isn't likely to be good for anybody.

#2985. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for their solid growth potential

Investors are closely monitoring earnings releases of artificial intelligence companies amid concerns about the durability of high spending and demand. While quarterly results provide key insights into a company's performance and execution, it is prudent to focus on long-term prospects and look beyond short-term fluctuations.

Top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick stocks with attractive long-term prospects as their assessments are backed by in-depth research of a company's fundamentals and growth opportunities.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

AI-powered data analytics provider Palantir Technologies is this week's first pick. The company reported market-beating second-quarter results. Moreover, Palantir raised its full-year guidance and now expects U.S. commercial business revenue to grow by at least 134%.

Following the Q2 print, Bank of America analyst Mariana Perez Mora reiterated a buy rating on PLTR stock with a price target of $255. Mora said that Palantir's performance reflects the success of its AI strategy, which focuses on delivering the right infrastructure to customers and ensuring better outcomes.

"The main driver continues to be the U.S. commercial business, which now represents almost 40% of total (vs. 30% a year ago and just 20% a couple of years back)," noted Mora.

The five-star analyst highlighted the 149% growth in Palantir's U.S. commercial business in Q2 2026 and the expansion in its customer base and engagement levels, with clients pursuing more integrated relationships. Mora noted that the U.S. commercial customer count increased 35% year-over-year to 653, with trailing twelve months revenue per customer surging 76% to $3.5 million in the second quarter.

Citing strength across Palantir's commercial and government segments, Mora increased her sales and earnings per share estimates for 2026-2028. The analyst attributed the increase in her estimates to the growth in the commercial business, backed by stronger total contract value in the second quarter.

Mora ranks No. 444 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 32.6%. See Palantir Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

E-commerce and cloud computing giant Amazon impressed investors with its strong second-quarter results. Revenue from the company's Amazon Web Services (AWS) cloud business surged 37%, marking the unit's fastest growth since 2021.

In reaction to the Q2 2026 performance, JPMorgan analyst Doug Anmuth reiterated a buy rating on Amazon stock and raised his price target to $365 from $330, saying that it remains the firm's Best Idea. Anmuth highlighted that the company's forex-neutral top-line growth accelerated to 20% in Q2 2026, the fastest in 20 quarters. He added that the top line gained from acceleration across both AWS and stores.

The five-star analyst noted that AWS growth of 37% was the fastest in 18 quarters, with backlog jumping nearly 2.5x year-over-year and 36% sequentially to $496 billion. Amazon's backlog gained from robust demand across both core workloads and AI adoption. Anmuth also observed the strength in AWS' AI and chips businesses, Graviton revenue commitments, and AWS' core business.

Notably, Anmuth believes that the core AWS business is highly correlated to AI revenue, and he expects further strengthening of this relationship as more AI workloads move into full-scale production and fuel higher demand. Overall, the analyst is more optimistic on Amazon following Q2 results and raised his 2026 and 2027 sales estimates by about 1% to 2% and operating income by about 5% to 7%.

"Importantly, management emphasized that the [return on invested capital] on its AI investments is compelling and it has a clear line-of-sight to strong financial returns," said Anmuth.

Anmuth ranks No. 717 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.5%. See Amazon Statistics on TipRanks.

In reaction to the solid results, Oppenheimer analyst Edward Yang reaffirmed a buy rating on LRCX stock with a price target of $400. #2982. What You Can Pay for with Scholarship Money

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships are usually restricted to the necessary costs of attending college, but some scholarships let you apply the money to more than tuition.

Scholarships are meant to pay for the cost of attendance to study at a college or university. This often includes required bills and purchases, such as tuition, textbooks and on-campus living expenses.

Some scholarships restrict the funds to specific costs, such as tuition. How you can apply the money may depend on the scholarship’s requirements.

Here are the most common college costs you can pay with scholarship money.

Scholarships commonly pay toward the tuition and fees your college charges.

Tuition usually covers only the cost to enroll in classes. Your college will also charge fees for a variety of reasons, such as attending a lab portion of a class, access to on-campus technology, facility maintenance and student activities. You’ll have to pay many of these fees regardless of if you use those facilities or participate in campus activities. Fees are often charged with tuition on the same bill so that you pay them together.

Tuition and fees cost students at public four-year institutions an average of $32,730 for the 2026-2027 school year, according to College Board’s 2026 report. If you use scholarship money to pay for these costs upfront, it can help you save a good chunk of money.

Scholarship money also pays for textbooks and supplies you need to complete your courses. These can include physical books, e-books and software licenses to access online content.

Depending on the scholarship, the cost of additional supplies that are required for certain courses might be covered as well. For example, nursing students might have to purchase scrubs with their college’s logo or medical supplies such as stethoscopes for clinical shifts.

Some scholarships will pay for the purchase of a laptop because you’ll likely need one to complete your coursework; however, you’ll want to confirm that the money can be used for that purchase before buying one.

Your scholarship might help cover some of the costs of living on campus, including paying for a dorm room and meal plans (often called room and board). To apply the funds, you usually have to live in university housing, which might be in a dormitory or even university apartments.

Many students can purchase meal plans to eat meals in their college and university cafeterias. These plans are usually for a set number of meals each week and might be required for first-year students. Because meal plans are part of college expenses, some scholarships will let you apply the funds to purchase a meal plan.

Some scholarships might also allow money to pay for campus parking fees because those are paid directly to the college or university.

» MORE: What is need-based financial aid for college?

Scholarships are generally intended only for educational expenses such as those explained above, but there are some scholarships that want you to use the money for specific expenses. If your scholarship has this type of restriction, your award letter should outline how the funds must be used, such as paying for tuition and fees only.

If you aren’t certain whether there are restrictions on how you can spend your scholarship money and the scholarship was paid directly to your college, you can call your financial aid office to ask. But you’ll want to contact the organization that awarded you the scholarship to ask about restrictions if the scholarship money was sent to you instead.

Even if a scholarship is open to most educational costs, expenses that are rarely allowed to be covered by a scholarship include:

Some transportation costs such as car payments.

Non-essential supplies such as clothing or entertainment.

Scholarships can be paid directly to your college or to you, depending on the individual scholarship. If the money goes to your college, it’s applied to your account based on the scholarship’s requirements. For example, if the funds are only allowed to pay for tuition, the financial aid office will apply the money to your tuition bill to reduce how much you owe.

Scholarships work the same way for room and board. If your scholarship will pay toward the costs of living on campus, the money will reduce how much you owe or cover the costs entirely so that you don’t owe anything.

In some cases, you might be able to use your scholarship money to pay for books or supplies. If that’s allowed, the funds might be available on your account through the college bookstore. Instead of paying the entire cost of your textbooks with cash or your student loans, you can pay with the scholarship money.

If your scholarship sends you the money instead of giving it to your college, you’ll be responsible for using the funds to pay for eligible costs instead of your university automatically applying it toward your bill.

#2980. 4 Things the Headlines Missed in Nvidia's First Post-Jensen Earnings Report

Nvidia easily beat third-quarter expectations, yet the real story is hiding in the fine print. Interim CEO Colette Kress just dialed back the sales swagger and put customer financing on a tighter leash.

By Anders Bylund – Nov 18, 2026 at 10:47PM EST

The leather jacket is gone, and the spreadsheets are officially in charge.

Reporting its first quarterly results just days after former CEO Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation, Nvidia (NVDA -2.90%) proved that its AI dominance doesn't depend on one charismatic ringmaster. In Q3 of fiscal year 2027, Nvidia's revenue soared 103% year over year to $115.8 billion, the new Vera Rubin architecture racked up nearly $20 billion in sales in its debut quarter, and interim CEO Colette Kress even raised the company's fiscal 2028 revenue growth target from 70% to 75%.

But beneath those breathtaking headline numbers, interim CEO Colette Kress delivered the report with the measured calm you expect from a 13-year veteran CFO. Kress toned down some of her predecessor's boldest growth framing while tightening the guardrails around how Nvidia deploys its capital.

It's a serious top-level strategy shift, brought on by an unexpected scandal. You can see most of it in the earnings release and prepared CFO comments without even digging into the earnings call.

And I think I like Kress's attitude. For the first time in forever, Nvidia's business plan makes sense to me.

Back in the second quarter, Nvidia couldn't resist hyping up its $105 billion lease guarantee for OpenAI's massive Ohio AI data center campus, telling investors that each generation of chips installed there could generate $150 billion to $200 billion in Nvidia revenue.

In her debut quarter at the helm, Kress deleted that $200 billion sales pitch from the report. Instead, she offered a step-by-step breakdown of how Nvidia's credit guarantee will take effect across nine separate buildings starting in fiscal year 2029. She also reassured investors by noting that Nvidia hasn't yet paid out any of its financing guarantees. The expected growth is still record-breaking, but on a scale from 1 to 10, Kress dialed back the marketing swagger from 11 to 6.

A longtime CFO could be expected to starve Nvidia's growth engine in order to hoard cash. The supply chain numbers say that Nvidia will have plenty of growth fuel under new management.

Kress lifted Nvidia's multi-year supply and memory chip commitments from $279 billion in Q2 to $390 billion in Q3. The boost springs from incredible demand for the Vera Rubin AI accelerator.

Where did she tighten things up? That would be in the customer financing area. Reported additional commitments related to cash-burning "neocloud" start-ups fell from $56 billion to $32 billion. A new warning in the reporting boilerplate said that those commitments can be "reduced or eliminated" if the neoclouds fail to line up their own financing. And on the call, Kress noted that the underlying deal terms haven't changed; Nvidia will simply be "disciplined and selective" about these contracts. Non-investment-grade customers had better have their capital and end buyers lined up before Nvidia commits to shipping them GPUs from a scarce supply.

To gauge Nvidia's financial health, Wall Street spends hours and hours analyzing the capital spending budgets of Microsoft, Alphabet, and Meta Platforms. Hyperscalers are no longer the fastest-growing part of the business, though. And they may soon become the smaller slice, too.

Hyperscale revenue climbed 71% year over year to $60.1 billion, but Nvidia's AI Clouds, Industrial, and Enterprise segment roughly tripled. Sales in this segment jumped 195% to $47.5 billion. The roster of non-tech-giant customers is broadening fast, including sovereign AI initiatives, enterprise quantitative trading clusters, and stand-alone Vera CPU sales.

With Nvidia currently ringing up nearly $10 billion in sales every week, that extra calendar week accounts for more than 60% of the sequential revenue jump between Q3 and Q4. The underlying demand for Nvidia's AI silicon is definitely strong. Still, investors should remember that the calendar is giving Q4 a temporary tailwind this year -- and next year's 13-week fourth quarter will face the same calendar quirk in reverse.

#2977. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The stock market continues to grapple with volatility amid geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and concerns about the sustainability of the AI boom. Given this uncertainty, investors looking for steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick the right dividend stocks, as these experts assign their ratings after an in-depth analysis of a company's financials and ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Downstream energy company Phillips 66 (PSX) is this week's first dividend pick. With a quarterly dividend $1.27 per share (annualized dividend of $5.08), PSX offers a yield of 2.25%. The company recently reported solid Q2 earnings, as the Middle East conflict affected global supplies and drove up refining margins.

Following the Q2 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Phillips 66 and raised his price target to $255 from $240, reflecting higher 2026 earnings expectations and lower interest expense next year.

Gabelman highlighted the quarter-over-quarter reduction in Phillips 66's net debt, noting management's optimism at achieving its estimated $15.5 billion net debt target one year ahead of schedule. The 5-star analyst expects the company to end 2026 with a net debt of $14.6 billion.

"The [balance sheet] improvement could re-establish PSX as a go-to defensive refiner," said Gabelman.

Management agreed that PSX's payout ratio lagged year-to-date, but expects buybacks to increase in the second half. Gabelman said Phillps indicated the possibility of a larger dividend hike following an annual increase of 5% over the past two years.

Gabelman ranks No. 554 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Phillips 66 Statistics on TipRanks.

Crescent Energy (CRGY) is an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian and Uinta Basins. Earlier this month, the company reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings and announced a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share, payable on August 31. At an annualized dividend of $0.48 per share, CRGY boasts a dividend yield of about 4%.

In reaction to Q2 results, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reaffirmed a buy rating on Crescent Energy stock with a price target of $18. He noted continued strength in the company's performance, with second-quarter oil production and cash flow surpassing the Street's expectations.

"CRGY's cash flow exceeded expectations by 10%, reinforcing its trajectory of capital efficiency," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that Crescent raised its full-year oil production guidance, citing the effective integration of the Vital Energy acquisition. Specifically, Crescent tripled its synergy target from the Vital Energy deal to as much as $300 million, significantly reducing the effective purchase price. This reflects Crescent's solid execution following the acquisition, Richardson said.

Additionally, the analyst noted that Crescent's capital spending is and heading toward the lower end of management's prior guidance, indicating disciplined financial controls.

Richardson ranks No. 579 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.5%. See Crescent Energy Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Finally, Viper Energy (VNOM) is effectively controlled by Diamondback Energy. Viper owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests in oil-weighted basins, mainly the Permian in West Texas. Viper recently announced its Q2 2026 results and declared a 32% hike in its base dividend, effective in the third quarter of 2026. The new base dividend indicates a 4.5% annualized yield.

Viper also removed its quarterly commitment to return at least 75% of cash available for distribution. The company believes that this move will ensure increased flexibility to make opportunistic share repurchases and pursue accretive mergers and acquisitions.

Following the Q2 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper, slightly raising his price target to $59 from $58. Bilkoski said Viper's solid Q2 performance supports his view that the strength in oil prices and rising operator activity are driving higher production.

"Viper has delivered, and we forecast will continue to deliver, one of the highest production per share growth profiles in our royalty universe" through the end of 2027, said Bilkoski. He believes Viper deserves a premium valuation, given its above-average growth.

The 5-star analyst said that while the biggest surprise in the quarter was Viper's revision to its shareholder return framework, he doesn't believe that the move indicates a significant change to its underlying strategy. Under the new framework, a larger portion of excess free cash flow is expected to be allocated to share repurchases than to variable dividends, Bilkoski said.

Bilkoski ranks No. 719 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Viper Energy Financials on TipRanks.
