#2974. The S&P 500 Just Ended a Run of 155 Trading Days Above Its 200-Day Average. Here's What Followed 34 Similar Runs Since 1950.

The index just ended a 155-session run above that line. What came after the 34 earlier runs of 150-plus sessions?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 18, 2026 at 11:04PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.23%) finished Tuesday, Nov. 17, at 7,340.53. That left it a few points under its 200-day moving average (the average of its past 200 daily closes, or around nine and a half months of trading), which was near 7,345.

It was the index's first close below that line since April 7. The next day, the S&P 500 began a run of 155 straight sessions above its average, posting a record close of 7,818.93 on Oct. 6 along the way.

Its worst day since that high was Nov. 5, when it dropped over 4% as chipmakers and other stocks linked to artificial intelligence (AI) sold off. As I write, the index is roughly 6% below its record. That still puts it about 7% above where it closed 2025.

With tech stocks leading the slide, a break like this may look like the market's longer-term trend turning down. Does it normally bring more trouble?

I went through the index's history since 1950 and found 34 earlier times a run of at least 150 sessions above the 200-day average ended. Three months later, the index was up in 26 of them.

My numbers come from Yahoo Finance's record of the index's daily closes since January 1950. These cover only price changes, not dividends. For every day, I averaged the 200 most recent closes, then counted how many sessions in a row the index finished above its average.

The longest run, at 628 sessions, ended in May 1956. Notably, four of the last six breaks happened this decade on Feb. 27, 2020, Jan. 21, 2022, March 10, 2025, and March 19 this year.

After the March break, the index dropped about 4% further in the next seven sessions, closed back above its average on April 8, and was around 14% higher by mid-June.

In half the 34 cases, the index closed back above its 200-day average within five trading days, and in 28 of them, it did so within 20 sessions (about four weeks). But the 1987 break was the big outlier -- the index took 161 sessions to get back above its average.

Across all 34 breaks, the index climbed over the next three months 76% of the time, with a median gain of around 4%. By comparison, a three-month stretch from any random trading day since 1950 finished with a gain in about two out of three cases (67%), with a median gain closer to 3%. In other words, the months after a break have arguably been a bit better than an ordinary stretch.

Still, the exceptions were rough. The worst came after April 4, 1962, when the index dropped about 17% over the next three months.

Another break came on Oct. 15, 1987, two sessions before the crash of Oct. 19, when the index dropped about 20% in one day. Three months after that break, it was still down roughly 15%.

The year after a break looks even more like an ordinary year. Of the 33 breaks that are at least a year old, the index ended the next 12 months up in 23, or around 70%, and the median gain was about 9%. In a normal 12-month period since 1950, it's climbed about 75% of the time, with a median gain of about 11%.

Of course, a break didn't always mean a quiet year. In 11 of the 33 cases, the index slid another 10% or more below its break-day close at some point within 12 months. That set includes August 2007, when the index was down around 13% a year later.

But it also includes February 2020 and March 2025. Both times, the index fell further within weeks, by around 25% through March 23, 2020, and about 11% through April 8, 2025. And both times, it ended the next 12 months higher, up about 28% and 21%, respectively.

So, should a long-term holder of a fund tracking the S&P 500 sell after a break like this? I don't think so.

In most of these cases, selling would've meant missing gains in the months that followed.

And on the day of each break, the ones that turned into deeper drops didn't look very different from the rest. At the median, the index stood around 8% under its high of the previous year in those cases, against about 7% in the others.

I'd keep holding, though I think the index could easily fall more from here.

#2973. I Didn’t Know Southwest Points Could Be This Valuable

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Some of the best Southwest redemptions are only available if you hold one of the airline’s credit cards.

In a world of transferable points currencies, you might not think holding an airline or hotel credit card is necessary. But travel brands are pushing back against that narrative, offering exclusive perks and redemption packages to those who carry their cards.

Take Southwest Airlines, for example. Its Rapid Rewards loyalty program allows you to redeem points for flights, but it also offers a special program called Rapid Rewards Access Events exclusively for Southwest credit cardholders. In exchange for a decent chunk of points, you can get a vacation package that’s worth significantly more than the price you pay. Instead of a traditional flight award redemption, you get a cohesive travel experience.

"Experiences create lifetime memories,” says Nandika Suri, Southwest’s vice president of Rapid Rewards. “For us, we really want them to have great memories and remember our brand as part of that.”

Southwest invited me to participate in one of its Rapid Rewards Access Events. While Southwest covered the cost of my trip, these opinions are my own. Here’s what I learned.

Every event is different (ranging from concerts to multiday trips), but big-ticket Rapid Rewards Access Events tend to include flights, hotel accommodations, meals and excursions.

The event I participated in was in Maui to celebrate the 30th anniversary of Southwest and Chase’s partnership. This was a bigger production than the typical event, with about 60 cardholders (and a plus one) participating. A typical multiday event might have 20 or 25 packages for sale.

In exchange for 275,000 Rapid Rewards points, cardholders received for themselves and one guest:

A four-night stay in an ocean-view room at the Grand Wailea, a Waldorf Astoria Resort, in Maui, including daily breakfast.

Ground transportation between the airport and the resort, as well as for any scheduled group activities.

A special welcome reception and farewell dinner.

A snorkeling cruise with lunch and beverages included.

A choice between several excursions, including a spa treatment, golf, ziplining and more.

Even at extremely conservative estimates, I would value all of those things at roughly $4,600. That’s $750 more than what NerdWallet values 275,000 Rapid Rewards points at, according to our 2026 data. If we took a more liberal approach, I think it would be fair to say the package was worth more than $7,000. That puts this redemption between 1.7 and 2.5 cents per point. (Expand the section below to see how I arrived at those numbers.)

As of 2026, NerdWallet values Southwest Rapid Rewards points at 1.4 cents apiece. That puts the 275,000-point Southwest event package at $3,850.

Let’s put some numbers to the package inclusions and see how the points price stacks up to the cash valuation.

$1,000 Southwest gift card: It’s pretty easy to value a $1,000 Southwest gift card at $1,000. You need to fly to the event, so that can be taken at face value. (You could also use it toward a different trip, if you wanted to use points or fly with a different carrier.)

Four-night hotel stay: For the Grand Wailea stay, a nonrefundable Hilton Honors discount rate for an ocean-view room on the exact same dates in 2027 costs $4,490.19, after factoring in taxes and fees. (The base-level terrace-view room would cost $3,992.17.)

If you were to use points, a standard room without an ocean view would cost 130,000 Hilton points per night. An ocean-view room’s rate shoots up to 281,000 Hilton points per night.

Using NerdWallet’s 2026 valuation of Hilton points at 0.4 cent per point, that puts the value of the two rooms at $2,080 and $4,496, respectively. To make things interesting, let’s say the hotel stay is worth $2,080.

Daily breakfast at the hotel buffet is about $60 per person, before tax and tip. For four days, let’s put that at around another $500, bringing the total stay value to $2,580.

Ground transportation: Let’s make things simple and just say it’s $30 each way between the airport and the resort. For two people, that would be worth $120.

Welcome reception and farewell dinner: Southwest and Chase put a lot of effort into making these events nice, from the food and free-flowing alcohol to the entertainment (there was even a fire performance at the farewell dinner).

I would value this at $50 per person, conservatively, or $200 total.

Snorkeling cruise: I went on Viator to find a similar experience, and the cheapest option I found was priced at $100 per person.

Again, let’s price it conservatively and say it’s worth $200. That price doesn't include hotel transportation, gratuities, towels or alcoholic beverages, while the Southwest package did.

Excursion choice: Southwest let everyone choose an excursion, ranging from a chill pool or spa day to ziplining through the jungle or learning to surf.

It’s hard to put a number on this, because the activities varied in cost.

I went ziplining, and it was $240 for six lines. That price doesn’t include transportation to the excursion, which was covered by Southwest. Meanwhile, a standard massage at the hotel spa is $275 (and that doesn’t even factor in the 20% service charge).

Let’s put this at $250 per person, giving us a round $500 for two people’s excursions.

Grand total: Summing up these numbers gets us $4,600. That’s $750 more than the $3,850 NerdWallet values 275,000 points at, but keep in mind that we probably undervalued several of the inclusions. The actual value of a trip like this could be much higher.

It also doesn’t factor in other things, like snacks provided at the hospitality booth, as well as surprise gifts and goodie bags prepared by Southwest and Chase for attendees.

Before you book a Rapid Rewards Access Event, there are some drawbacks to booking a vacation this way.

The biggest one is if you don’t like group trips or following a schedule on your vacations. Given the nature of these events, you’ll be surrounded by a group of travelers, many of whom will likely be longtime Southwest flyers. If you prefer to keep to yourself, then this type of trip is likely not a good fit.

You’ll also have multiple scheduled activities on your itinerary. While you can choose to not show up, those activities add a lot of value to the trip.

With that said, I found the itinerary quite laid back. The amount of scheduled activities wasn’t overwhelming, and there was plenty of free time to do whatever you wanted. One couple I met even rented a car and explored Maui in their downtime, so they kept themselves busy.

These events are extremely popular, and many attendees I met at the Maui event had been on multiple Southwest cardmember trips.

Scoring a ticket to a Rapid Rewards Access Event might even require setting your alarm for when tickets drop. Several cardmembers told me they’ve experienced times when the event they wanted was sold out in hours, if not minutes. (The Maui one took a few hours to sell out, because it was larger than most.)

Finally, 275,000 points is a lot — even if you're getting great value. If your Rapid Rewards points are collecting dust in your account, then it could make sense. But if you have a transferable currency, then you'd have to weigh whether you might get better value elsewhere, such as booking round-trip business class flights.

Southwest isn’t the only airline or travel brand to offer special events for cardholders. United Airlines has MileagePlus Exclusives and United Card Events from Chase; Delta Air Lines offers cardmember-exclusive redemptions in its SkyMiles Experiences program; and Marriott has its Marriott Bonvoy Moments program.

But every redemption option is different — even if it’s within the same loyalty program. For example, some Rapid Rewards Access Events are packages you can purchase immediately for a set price, while others are auction-style listings. Some programs offer paid experiences, so you can only pay in cash, not points.

Personally, I’m a fan of the fixed-price points option. You know the upfront price, and you don’t have to worry about getting into a last-minute bidding war that could lead you to paying more than you intended. Plus, Southwest has historically had limited award redemption options, although that will change in 2027 when it allows international partner award bookings.

Ultimately, whether you think a Rapid Rewards Access Event is a worthwhile redemption will depend on your interests, and Southwest is working to expand its offerings to meet people’s passions.

"We do offer a lot of breadth because we want to get different types of customers engaged with different types of things based on their individual passion points and what they're really interested in,” says Suri, the Rapid Rewards chief.

If you’d rather spend time in the mountains than on the beach, you might not want to spend 275,000 Rapid Rewards points on the Maui package.

Instead, you probably would've been interested in the 250,000-point package for "Boots, Bourbon and Bonfires" — a three-day trip to Devil's Thumb Ranch, an upscale dude ranch resort about 1.5 hours outside of Denver.

Before you book, just remember: You’ll need a Southwest credit card to purchase.

#2965. Magnite vs. The Trade Desk: Which Ad Tech Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Two ad-tech leaders, two very different business models, and a 2027 pick that depends on which side of the programmatic ad transaction appears stronger right now.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 18, 2026 at 11:36PM EST

Automated, or programmatic, advertising is reshaping the media landscape by providing more transparency and efficiency for both ad buyers and sellers. Choosing between Magnite (MGNI -2.46%) and The Trade Desk (TTD +3.95%) requires deciding which side of the transaction offers the most potential.

Magnite focuses on helping media owners maximize the value of their digital inventory, while The Trade Desk empowers agencies and brands to buy that inventory effectively. Both companies are at the center of the shift toward programmatic advertising, making them essential players in the evolving digital ecosystem.

Magnite operates in the digital advertising market, with a strong presence among online publishers and content providers requiring automated tools to sell inventory. As a supply-side platform, the company provides the technology that media owners use to manage their ad space. Two major buyers indirectly contributed nearly 44% of its revenue in 2025. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached $766.7 million, representing growth of 9.1% compared to the prior year. The company reported net income of $180.8 million during this period. The performance reflects a steady expansion in its ability to monetize video and connected TV inventory across global markets.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was 0.7x. This ratio compares total debt to shareholder equity to show how much leverage a company uses. The current ratio, which measures if a company can cover its short-term obligations with its current assets, was approximately 1.0x. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was $165.6 million. Note that stock-based compensation (SBC) represented 32.5% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

The Trade Desk provides a self-service, cloud-based platform that allows advertising buyers to manage data-driven campaigns across multiple devices. The company primarily serves advertising agencies and major brands that need to optimize their spending in real time. In 2025, two unnamed holding companies each represented more than 10% of the company's gross billings. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue was $3.0 billion, which was a 7% increase over the previous 12-month period. Net income for that same time frame reached $329.4 million. This steady top-line growth is supported by its broad network of hundreds of integrated ad exchanges and data vendors.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the company maintained a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.2x. The current ratio stood at 1.6x, indicating a solid buffer of liquid assets to handle short-term liabilities. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow reached $795.7 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented 49.4% of operating cash flow.

The Trade Desk trades at a significantly lower multiple of its sales, while Magnite offers a higher price relative to its future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Magnite and The Trade Desk represent opposite sides of the digital advertising coin. I believe the buy side, represented by The Trade Desk, is the stronger part of the ecosystem, as demonstrated by the rise of businesses such as Amazon. However, between these two ad tech giants, I recommend investing in Magnite right now.

The Trade Desk has been struggling as competition has intensified. Amazon and other new competitors battling for ad buying dollars, including many streaming services, have grown in recent years. Also, the artificial intelligence boom led to greater adoption of AI tools that appear to be eating into The Trade Desk's offerings.

The company exited 2025 with 18% year-over-year revenue growth to $2.9 billion, yet sales expansion has been elusive in 2026. In its latest quarterly earnings report, for the third quarter of 2026, The Trade Desk's sales fell 0.5% year over year to $736 million.

Magnite's business is headed in the opposite direction, as sales growth has accelerated. In Q1 of 2026, revenue rose 6% year over year. That jumped to 11% in Q2, then 14% in Q3. While the advent of new businesses into digital advertising has hurt The Trade Desk, this has helped Magnite, with streaming companies using its platform to monetize their content.

#2961. Should You Buy Take-Two Interactive After the GTA VI Release?

The long-awaited GTA VI video game is expected to deliver a "historic year" for Take-Two Interactive.

By Dave Kovaleski – Nov 19, 2026 at 12:01AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Take-Two Interactive Software ›

Gamers have been waiting more than a decade -- 13 years, to be exact -- for the latest edition of the popular Grand Theft Auto (GTA) video game franchise, and it's finally here. It's not just Christmas morning for GTA fans; it is like Christmas after getting coal in your stocking 13 years in a row.

GTA VI, produced by Take-Two Interactive (TTWO +6.22%), comes out on Nov. 19 at midnight in the Pacific time zone, and Take-Two's stock surged in advance of the release. That’s late Wednesday evening or early Thursday morning, depending on how you look at it. On Wednesday, Nov. 18, Take-Two stock jumped nearly 7% to around $220 per share, but it is still down about 15% year-to-date.

Is the release of GTA VI the catalyst that Take-Two stock needs to take off?

According to industry reports, GTA VI has received some 17.1 million pre-orders, with net revenue from the sales at $1.65 billion. This is reportedly the largest pre-order total for a video game ever.

On Take-Two's recent earnings call, CEO Strauss Zelnick said pre-orders "are tracking far in excess of our initial expectations."

For fiscal 2027, Take-Two anticipates net bookings of $8.0 billion to $8.2 billion -- that is revenue from games sold and in-game purchases. This would be up some 21% at the midpoint from the $6.7 billion in net bookings in fiscal 2026. The difference is largely due to the boost from GTA VI.

For perspective, GTA V, the last version (which came out in 2013), has sold 235 million copies, making it the second-best-selling video game of all time, behind Minecraft. The entire Grand Theft Auto franchise has sold about 480 million copies.

GTA is not Take-Two's only game franchise. It also owns the NBA 2K and Red Dead Redemption franchises, along with mobile games through Zynga, including Words with Friends, but GTA is its primary revenue driver.

For fiscal 2027, Take-Two raised its revenue guidance to $7.98 billion to $8.18 billion, up from $7.9 billion to $8.1 billion, and boosted its earnings per share range to $0.57 to $0.75, up from $0.55 to $0.75. It also expects to generate more than $1 billion in net cash, which will help it grow.

Zelnick said it will be a "historic year for our company," as it looks to "explore emerging business opportunities, expand our geographic footprint, and pursue accretive M&A."

Take-Two stock has dropped about 15% year-to-date as net bookings fell 18% to $1.6 billion in its Q2 while revenue plunged 15% year over year to $1.50 billion. The firm had a net loss of $54 million, or $0.29 per share last quarter.

But for Q3, it expects to double its net bookings to $3.35 billion to $3.5 billion, due mostly to GTA VI sales, while net income is anticipated to soar to $250 million to $276 million, or $1.30 to $1.44 per share.

Some 94% of Wall Street analysts rate Take-Two stock as a buy, with a median price target of $290 per share. That would suggest 32% upside.

With a forward price-to-earnings ratio of 28, Take-Two stock is not cheap, but it is not overvalued either, relative to its growth expectations. The GTA VI effect will have a multi-year impact on sales, as GTA V has shown, with analysts anticipating $9 billion in revenue in fiscal 2028, as a consensus.

There may be a sell-the-news dip, as some of the recent gains have already been priced in, given strong pre-order results and widespread anticipation. Since it dipped to $183 per share on Oct. 30, Take-Two stock has jumped about 20% to $220 per share. So perhaps it's best to allow the price to settle a bit. But in the longer term, Take-Two stock looks like a solid buy.

#2958. Ray Dalio says Bessent move is sign that a debt crisis is getting closer; recommends gold and bitcoin

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's debt buyback announcement this week fits into a broader pattern that can signal a forthcoming crisis, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.

The Bridgewater Associates founder said Bessent's plan to increase government debt purchases may portend trouble for the U.S. economy. Coupled with the Japanese government reducing its U.S. bond market exposure and surging long-dated American bond yields, Dalio said investors may want to prepare their portfolios for increased risk by owning cryptocurrencies and gold.

"I am confident that the government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote in a LinkedIn post published Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Dalio noted that the Treasury Department has "only limited capacity" to buy back bonds. Bessent told CNBC on Thursday that his team was going to "make a market" and that the purchases would likely top $4 billion.

The U.S. is spending about 40% more than it's bringing in, resulting in the burgeoning budget deficit, Dalio said. The U.S. budget deficit topped $432 billion in July, but Bessent told CNBC that it's likely peaked under President Donald Trump's administration.

Bessent said in the interview that a team was looking at ways to shrink spending by hundreds of billions of dollars. But Dalio said there is "very little ability" to do so given that it is either committed or considered essential.

After years of overspending, Dalio said total debt now dwarfs what the U.S. brings in each year. If the U.S. government was a business, Dalio said debt service payments would come in at roughly $11 trillion — about 200% of annual revenue.

Dalio, 77, warned the cost of repaying principal and servicing the debt will only grow over time.

To solve this, Dalio said the U.S. needs to carefully employ a three-part strategy to get the budget deficit down to 3% of gross domestic product.

First, Dalio said the U.S. government needs to reduce its spending. Second, he said tax revenue needs to be raised. Finally, the New York native and Harvard Business School alumni said lower interest rates were needed.

"All three need to happen concurrently so as to prevent any one from being too large," Dalio said. "If any one is too large, the adjustment will be traumatic."

Dalio cautioned against ramming through these adjustments by "force." For instance, "it would be very bad if the Federal Reserve unnaturally forced interest rates down," he said.

Dalio said it's important to to take steps now, while the economy is healthy. An economy in recession requires increased government spending, he said.

Dalio said the exact timing of a debt crisis can be swayed by variables ranging from military conflict to political change. On its current trajectory, the U.S. could enter such a crisis in as early as one year or as late as five. "My guess, which I suppose will be a bad one, is that it will come in three years, give or take two, if the course we're on is not changed."

To prepare, Dalio recommended investors are underweight debt assets, such as bonds.

As much as 10% to 15% of a portfolio could land in gold, as well as "a bit" of bitcoin, the hedge fund founder said.

Dalio's post came at the tail end of a volatile week for U.S. financial markets. Rising long-term Treasury yields have pressured stocks, leading the S&P 500 to snap a a three-week-long advance.

#2956. Why Anthropic Could Be a $5 Trillion Company By 2030

Investors have never seen a company like Anthropic before.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 19, 2026 at 12:19AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic, PBC ›

Anthropic (Nasdaq: ANT) is about to make stock market history.

The AI lab will go public on Thursday at a valuation of $1.5 trillion after pricing its IPO at $83 a share. While that is slightly below the $1.75 trillion that Space Exploration Technologies was valued at in its debut, Anthropic will raise more money than SpaceX, bringing in $107.3 billion, including a $26 private placement from some big tech companies.

Anthropic is just five years old, born from former OpenAI employees, and has experienced meteoric growth, with revenue up 1,200% through the first three quarters of the year. Its revenue run rate reached $68 billion at the end of the third quarter. No company in history has come close to that mark just five years since its inception.

Anthropic faces a lot of risks as it goes public. The company has approximately $900 billion in future purchase commitments for chips, AI compute, and lease obligations. It faces competition from other frontier AI labs like OpenAI as well as open-source and open-weight models, which could eat into its market share. Additionally, CEO Dario Amodei has highlighted the risks of AI and called for a slowdown in development to ensure adequate guardrails are in place.

Despite those risks, the company also has a lot of upside potential, and reaching a $5 trillion market cap is achievable. Here's why.

Anthropic might have more growth potential than any other company in history. We've already seen this anecdotally. Software stocks plunged at the beginning of the year when Anthropic introduced a series of plug-in tools for Claude Cowork, competing with specialized software for job functions like legal, finance, and sales.

The disruptive potential for Anthropic is virtually limitless, considering potential applications that extend to drug discovery, robotics, chip design, and beyond.

That's why it doesn't seem hyperbolic for the company to claim a market opportunity ranging from $22.5 trillion to $40 trillion. In its prospectus, Anthropic said that the International Data Corporation (IDC) estimates that AI will produce a $22.5 trillion impact by 2031, and Anthropic believes it could be even greater. Management also said that work performed by knowledge workers represented more than $40 trillion in economic value in 2025.

By both increasing knowledge worker productivity and shifting it to Claude, Anthropic certainly has a multi-trillion-dollar opportunity in front of it, which could support the stock tripling through 2030.

Anthropic has delivered incredible growth over the last year as its new frontier models have grabbed market share from OpenAI and driven a surge in Claude adoption.

In the third quarter, revenue jumped 1,127% year-over-year to $17 billion, rising 47% on a sequential basis. Over a year, 47% sequential growth would translate into 366% revenue growth on an annual basis. While we don't know what Anthropic's growth rate will be going forward, it clearly has the potential to deliver breakout growth if it releases new products and expands its addressable market.

Not only is Anthropic growing year-over-year revenue by more than 1,000%, but the company is now profitable on an adjusted basis with an adjusted operating profit of $4.1 billion through the first three quarters, and $2.5 billion in the third quarter. Anthropic's business is scaling as it grows, and its margins are expanding.

Based on the company's run rate revenue of $68 billion and the IPO valuation of $1.5 trillion, the stock is valued at 22 times sales, which looks like a bargain considering its growth rate and profitability.

It's not unusual for software stocks to trade at around 20 times sales, and the ones that do don't have the explosive growth or disruptive potential that Anthropic does, and some aren't even profitable on an adjusted basis.

With a massive addressable market and soaring growth rate, Anthropic could become a $5 trillion company sooner than investors expect, especially if it maintains a strong growth rate. Don't be fooled by the dialed-down IPO valuation.

#2953. GRAIL: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Promotional pricing trades near-term margin for adoption, and that bet only pays off if per-test costs fall as volumes scale.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 19, 2026 at 12:53AM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, GRAIL (GRAL -3.57%) highlighted a positive Food and Drug Administration (FDA) advisory committee vote for its Galleri multi-cancer early detection (MCED) test. FDA premarket approval (PMA), the agency's review process for high-risk medical devices, would create a distinct market class for Galleri.

It'd establish the test as the only approved screening tool that detects dozens of cancer types from one blood draw. Three things management said on the earnings call stand out for long-term investors.

An expert panel backed Galleri through the PMA review pathway in September. That vote positions the test to become the first FDA-approved multi-cancer screening tool. Management expects a final decision early in the first quarter of 2027, which would allow GRAIL to differentiate its product from unapproved competitors.

GRAIL introduced a promotional price in September to lower cost barriers in the self-pay market. That promotion pushed average selling price down to roughly $660 per test. Management is deliberately accepting lower gross margins today to expand provider adoption, build repeat testing habits, and prepare for insurance coverage.

Higher lab volumes should lower cost of goods sold (COGS) by about $100 per unit. That cost reduction helps offset a legacy royalty payment to Illumina that resumes in late December 2026. Management targets long-term adjusted gross margins of 50% to 60% as unit processing costs drop toward $230 at scale.

Commercial momentum's building across provider channels and electronic health record integrations. GRAIL's leaning on its clinical data set to secure coverage with private insurers and government payers.

A cash balance of $788.7 million provides financial runway into 2029 while GRAIL expands marketing and sales coverage. Management's aligning capital deployment to build repeat testing demand once insurance reimbursement opens.

#2949. Moderna's Cancer Vaccine Worked -- and the Stock Plunged Anyway. Here's What Investors Are Missing.

Moderna's melanoma vaccine plus Keytruda cut the risk of recurrence or death by 42% in Phase 3, yet the stock sank. Here's why.

By Rick Orford – Nov 19, 2026 at 1:00AM EST

Moderna (MRNA -4.84%) delivered promising results for its experimental cancer vaccine on Oct. 22, yet its stock plunged anyway. But looking past the headlines, there's more to the story than the trial results suggest, and investors may be overlooking the real opportunity.

At first glance, Moderna delivered the kind of clinical milestone that investors had been waiting for: positive Phase 3 results for its personalized melanoma vaccine, intismeran autogene, developed in partnership with Merck. But despite the solid results, Moderna shares fell more than 25% during the Oct. 22 session.

The sell-off followed an unexpected leak of the trial results ahead of their scheduled presentation at the European Society for Medical Oncology (ESMO) Congress. In the trial, the vaccine, when administered alongside Merck's Keytruda, reduced the risk of cancer recurrence or death by 42% compared with Keytruda alone.

On paper, these were encouraging results, but Moderna stock had already rallied following an earlier Phase 2 study. Indeed, this appears to be the case of the market wanting something even better.

For example, some analysts expected the Phase 3 hazard ratio to come in below 0.60, which would mean the vaccine reduces the risk of recurrence or death by more than 40%. Yet the actual result came in at 0.58, which technically cleared that threshold, but only narrowly.

In the end, given the high expectations, investors viewed the results as a disappointment. But there's more here than the headline numbers suggest.

Several analysts softened their initial views after the full presentation, recognizing that the vaccine delivered a meaningful improvement over Keytruda alone. The vaccine requires a series of personalized immunizations, which reduced the risk of recurrence or death by 58% based on outcomes six months or more after the initial treatment.

On top of that, the safety profile was notable. In fact, serious treatment-related adverse events occurred in around 10% of patients receiving the Moderna-Merck combination, compared with 30% in the separate NADINA trial of Opdivo and Yervoy.

That means Moderna's approach can potentially deliver additional clinical benefits without necessarily requiring patients to tolerate more aggressive immunotherapy combinations. As such, intismeran could still become an attractive option for patients less likely to tolerate dual checkpoint blockade, a cancer treatment that uses two different immunotherapy drugs.

Perhaps more importantly, though, Moderna's opportunity could be much bigger than melanoma.

Intismeran uses personalized mRNA technology to train a patient's immune system to recognize specific mutations associated with their cancer. Unlike conventional vaccines designed to prevent infections, this approach is intended to help the immune system identify and attack cancer cells that may otherwise evade detection.

It's also where the broader opportunity becomes something more: If the technology proves effective across different cancer types, Moderna could develop an entirely new category of personalized cancer treatments.

In fact, Moderna and Merck are already assessing intismeran's potential for other types of cancer, including renal cell carcinoma, bladder cancer, and non-small cell lung cancer. Plus, Moderna presented preliminary data from a study evaluating intismeran alongside chemotherapy in pancreatic cancer, with 55% of patients remaining relapse-free after 17 months of follow-up.

Despite these developments, intismeran still requires FDA approval, and overall survival data remain preliminary. On top of that, its effectiveness in other cancer types has yet to be proven.

Finally, today's valuation is also a concern. Right now, Moderna stock trades at a price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of about 35 times, so investors continue to have high expectations around future growth. Meanwhile, Merck trades at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of about 16.

Ultimately, at today's valuation, the market may be focusing too much on one trial result and not on Moderna's longer-term business.

Overall, Moderna's cancer vaccine may not have met Wall Street analysts' expectations, but it's still a meaningful clinical achievement. Looking ahead, investors need to be patient as the vaccine moves toward commercialization, but dismissing Moderna based on one trial result could mean overlooking a much bigger opportunity.

#2947. Top Wall Street analysts believe in the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Geopolitical uncertainty in the Middle East and soaring bond yields weighed on global markets this past week. And investors are closely assessing the valuations of artificial intelligence stocks amid concerns about the sustainability of high spending on AI infrastructure.

Despite those continued challenges, top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the prospects of several stocks due to their long-run growth potential and exposure to lucrative business lines, including AI.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD) is this week's first stock pick. The company is scheduled to announce fiscal second-quarter results on August 26.

Ahead of Q2 FY27 earnings, Truist analyst Junaid Siddiqui reiterated a buy rating on CrowdStrike stock and raised his price target to $245 from $187.50. Following CrowdStrike's impressive fiscal first-quarter performance and guidance raise, Siddiqui expects another strong quarter.

The 5-star analyst's optimism is backed by continued strength in Falcon Flex adoption and encouraging large deal activity. Siddiqui also cited continued momentum across emerging products. With the FY27 outlook assuming persistent strength in CrowdStrike's net new annual recurring revenue (ARR), Siddiqui is more focused on the longevity of key growth catalysts such as Falcon Flex adoption, platform consolidation and AI-led demand.

The analyst highlighted that talks with partners were encouraging, with pipeline generation and momentum in large deals remaining healthy in the fiscal second quarter despite tough, year-over-year comparisons.

"We continue to view CrowdStrike as one of the primary beneficiaries of platform consolidation, though we expect investors to remain focused on competitive dynamics and whether current growth levels represent a new baseline or a period of demand acceleration," the analyst said.

Siddiqui ranks No. 226 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 80% of the time, delivering an average return of about 40%. See CrowdStrike Financials on TipRanks.

Shares of personal computer maker Dell Technologies (DELL) have rallied 251% this year, driven by strong demand for AI servers. Recently, Evercore analyst Amit Daryanani reiterated a buy rating on DELL and raised his price target to $550 from $500.

The 5-star analyst contends that while Dell's AI compute segment is getting investors' attention, the company's Storage business is underappreciated and well-positioned to drive revenue and profit acceleration. Daryanani expects the storage business to see higher demand once enterprises start to bring workload back on-premises.

The analyst highlighted that Dell's Storage business, estimated to account for only 10% of FY27 revenue, is one of the factors that could help improve profit margins, given its higher profitability compared to AI servers. Daryanani estimates that the Storage business's operating margin is currently in the low-20% range, while margins for the AI server and traditional server businesses are in the mid-single-digit and mid-teens range, respectively.

Overall, Daryanani views the Storage business as an important catalyst for three reasons: Dell has one of the broadest storage portfolios in the industry; Storage has a significantly higher margin profile than AI servers; and the shift toward proprietary, software-defined storage and AI-focused platforms.

"We think DELL's storage business is becoming increasingly strategic as AI infrastructure deployments move beyond GPU procurement and toward full stack + AI-ready architectures," said Daryanani.

Daryanani ranks No. 19 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 38.70%. See Dell Technologies Statistics on TipRanks.

SanDisk (SNDK) is benefiting from AI-driven demand for its memory and storage products. Following the company's 2026 Investor Day event, J.P. Morgan analyst Harlan Sur resumed research coverage of SNDK with a buy rating (it was earlier, "Not Rated") and a price target of $2,250.

The 5-star analyst believes SanDisk is well-positioned to capitalize on a structural inflection reached in NAND demand, driven by strong growth in AI inference. Sur's bullishness is based on three key factors.

First, Sur believes that SanDisk's New Business Model (NBM) framework (or long-term agreements) has enhanced its margin profile and reduced cyclicality. SNDK's eight signed NBMs represent a total contract value of $94 billion and a gross margin of about 80%, even at the floor pricing.

Second, Sur highlighted the growing NAND market and expects the total addressable market to surge from about $70 billion in calendar year 2025 to over $300 billion in 2026 and about $500 billion in 2027. Importantly, the analyst noted that data centers are the largest driver of NAND growth, as hyperscalers increasingly deploy flash for AI inference workloads.

Finally, Sur is optimistic on SanDisk due to its technology dominance. The company's BiCS10 technology is sampling ahead of schedule and has 65% more bits per wafer than BiCS8, the analyst said. Also, Sur expects SanDisk's High Bandwidth Flash to become a differentiated memory platform for AI inference.

"Layered on top, mgmt's commitment to returning 100% of excess cash flow to shareholders should drive an aggressive share count reduction trajectory that further amplifies EPS growth on a per-share basis," said Sur.

Sur ranks No. 15 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 43.20%. See SanDisk Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2942. The 'debasement trade' returns after Bessent bond maneuver. Crypto and gold are back in style

The debasement trade is gaining new traction on Wall Street as concern over the size and cost of the budget deficit mounts.

The conventional wisdom behind the trade is that perceived hard assets, like cryptocurrencies and precious metals, gain as investors try to hedge against a weaker U.S. dollar and Treasury debt in the face of ballooning government spending. Those fears reached a fever pitch last week before, and after, the Treasury Department's unusual step of increasing debt buybacks under Secretary Scott Bessent.

"The size of the Treasury purchases announced so far by Bessent are trivial in comparison to the size of the overall market, but the [signaling] effect was very powerful," said Stephen Coltman, head of macro at 21Shares, a crypto-focused creator of exchange-traded funds.

Gold touched three-month highs Monday, building on last week's advance of more than 5%. The yellow metal has climbed for five straight weeks and in August is on track for its biggest monthly rise since 1999.

Bitcoin added 2% Monday, to the highest since May. Last week, the digital currency soared 22%, its biggest three-day rally since 2023. Overnight Tuesday, the crypto touched $80,000.

The U.S. dollar index, which tracks the dollar against six other leading currencies, hit three-month lows last week and recorded its third down week in the last four. The index was little changed on Monday as investors expressed little appetite to bid up the greenback.

The Treasury Department said last week that it would double the maximum size of its bond buyback, to at least $4 billion from $2 billion. Two senior Treasury officials told CNBC Monday that the department could use its General Account to help fund the plans.

Last week's announcement followed news that the monthly U.S. budget deficit in July reached a five-year high and came at the same time as total federal government debt topped $40 trillion. Bessent told CNBC last week that he wields a "big toolkit" to calm the government bond market amid concerns about the government's financial health.

Long-dated U.S. Treasury yields surged last week, at one point sending the 30-year yield to almost a 20-year high of 5.34%, up from 4.82% in late June. Yields dipped and then rebounded in the wake of the Treasury buyback move, a signal that bond investors believed Bessent's moves were inadequate.

"Markets are saying something," the billionaire philanthropist and former energy trader John Arnold said in a Friday post on X. The weaker dollar, lower Treasury prices and strengthening hard assets, are "all part of the debasement trade," he wrote.

The Treasury's moves could aid the bond market, but also translate into outsized pain for the dollar, according to Nohshad Shah, Citadel Securities' head of fixed income sales for Europe, the Middle East and Africa.

The stakes are high for monetary policymakers, as a weaker greenback can ease financial conditions, Shah said. U.S. inflation — above the Federal Reserve's 2% target for five years — could worsen.

The Fed might need to hike interest rates in response, Shah said. Fed funds futures reflect about a 56% chance the central bank raises borrowing costs at its October meeting, up more than seven percentage points from a week ago, according to CME's FedWatch tool.

"The bond market's message is straightforward: fiscal or monetary policy should be tighter," Shah said in a Monday note. "Households may ultimately pay for policymakers' unwillingness to fix the roof whilst the sun is shining."

Coltman at 21Shares said the appetite for alternative stores of value also stems from mounting geopolitical tension. The U.S. on Monday rolled out a global sanctions program focused on segregating Iran from the global economy, days after slapping 50% tariffs on billions of dollars in Canadian exports.

Still, assets that would benefit from a debasement trade have recently received votes of confidence on Wall Street.

Deutsche Bank analyst Michael Hsueh said in a Monday note that gold could surpass his target price of $4,800 an ounce. Already, that level suggests the precious metal only need to rise another 3% or so from where it closed on Friday. "We see the Treasury policy change as underlining the gold constructive view," Hsueh said.

Billionaire investor Ray Dalio recommended investors remain overweight gold and bitcoin ahead of what he warned could become a debt crisis in the U.S. as a result of unchecked government spending. Gold could account for as much as 15% of a model portfolio, the Bridgewater Associates founder said.

"The government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote on LinkedIn Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Others are less sure, devaluing the notion of a debasement trade.

It's probably too early to endorse the idea unless it's clear that the Fed will go along with the Treasury Department, said Alexander Lis, investing chief at Social Discovery Ventures.

#2940. Planet Labs Teams Up With Stoke Space -- Just In Case SpaceX Really Retires the Falcon 9

If SpaceX retires the Falcon 9, Planet Labs will be ready with a replacement ride to space.

By Rich Smith – Nov 19, 2026 at 1:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Planet Labs PBC ›

Planet Labs owns the world's biggest constellation of Earth observation satellites -- approximately 200 satellites in orbit, comprising the Dove and SkySat constellations that capture over 25 terabytes of imagery each day. These satellites only last about three to five years in orbit, however, before they fall out of the sky and must be replaced. This means that, even without growing its constellation size, Planet Labs needs to continually launch rockets with new satellites to refresh its fleet.

And here's the problem: Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.27%), the company that launches most of these satellites on its Falcon 9 rockets, is planning to retire those vehicles.

I argued recently that once SpaceX realizes how big of a problem it's creating for other space companies by retiring its Falcon -- and how much money it could make if it keeps Falcon operating -- SpaceX might reconsider and postpone Falcon's retirement.

But Planet Labs is making contingency plans. The company announced it has signed a multi-launch agreement with private space start-up Stoke Space Technologies.

As Planet Labs explains, its contract calls for Stoke to carry Planet Labs' satellites atop 15-ton-payload Stoke Nova reusable rockets, which will begin launching from Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 14 (SLC-14) in 2027.

No price was specified in the contract announcement, so we don't know exactly how much Planet Labs will pay for these launches. It's also not 100% clear when Stoke will launch the first Planet satellites.

Stoke has announced an early 2027 target date for the 1.0 version of Nova, which it is calling Nova Pathfinder. The first launch will carry payloads from space-mining company AstroForge and space-cremation company Celestis, which may leave little room for Planet Labs’ satellites.

Additional Nova Pathfinder missions are planned throughout 2027 and 2028, however, before Stoke transitions to a larger model: Nova Block 2. And even before Block 2 arrives, the original recipe Nova Pathfinder rocket is expected to have a 3-ton payload to low earth orbit -- more than enough to replace Planet Labs' entire SuperDove constellation in one go.

Long story short, Payload appears to have found itself a workable solution to the problem of vanishing Falcon 9 rockets. If Falcon missions do end in 2028 as expected, and Stoke succeeds in getting Nova operational this year -- or even next year or the year after that -- it should arrive in time for Planet Labs to avoid any SpaceX-related complications to its business plan.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

#2937. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Inflation data, earnings reports and tensions in the Middle East have been influencing the market. In this volatile backdrop, investors seeking income can bolster their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

The recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help inform investors in their search for attractive stocks from the vast universe of dividend-paying companies. These experts assign ratings after analyzing a company's financials and ability to consistently pay dividends while also seeking capital appreciation.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Exxon Mobil has increased its dividends for 43 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.03 per share (annualized dividend of $4.12), XOM stock offers a yield of 2.6%.

In his latest research report on energy players, Morgan Stanley analyst Devin McDermott reiterated a buy rating on Exxon Mobil stock and increased his price target to $177 from $168.

The analyst raised his free cash flow estimates to reflect Q2 guidance and the oil price strip, with the largest increase noted for integrated players due to higher refining margins. Notably, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow estimates by an average of 20% for the second half of 2026 and 7% for 2027.

McDermott highlighted that while pure-play refiners have rallied on stronger margins, integrated energy stocks have lagged, creating relative-value opportunities in major North American integrated producers, including Exxon Mobil, Suncor and Cenovus Energy.

Overall, McDermott is bullish on Exxon Mobil, as he expects the company to deliver strong earnings and cash flow growth, driven by robust upstream volumes, high-value projects in Product Solutions, and more than $5 billion of additional structural cost savings.

"XOM's large global integrated operations and strong balance sheet (0.0x 2027 net leverage) make it a clear defensive play in an uncertain macro backdrop," said McDermott.

McDermott ranks No. 726 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.80%. See Exxon Mobil Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to natural gas producer Expand Energy. The company announced a quarterly base dividend of $0.575 per share to be paid on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend of $2.30 per share, EXE offers a yield of about 2.3%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on Expand Energy stock and increased his price target to $113 from $99. Mehta attributed his higher price target to cash flow improvement and a strong outlook for shareholder returns. He also cited the stock's attractive valuation, with an 11% free cash flow, or FCF, yield based on his 2027/2028 estimates compared with the peer average of 9%.

The five-star analyst expects Expand Energy to generate FCF per share of about $10 in FY28, up from the prior estimate of about $8 at his mid-cycle price forecast of $3.50/MMBtu Henry Hub. Mehta raised his FCF estimate to reflect an improved outlook for share repurchases, operational efficiencies, and gas pricing realizations.

Furthermore, the analyst expects the recently announced $1.25 billion Twin Eagle acquisition to enhance the competitive positioning of the combined business to expand to premium markets and capture incremental deals in the power and LNG end markets.

Mehta ranks No. 449 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.1%. See Expand Energy Insider Trading on TipRanks.

Mehta is also bullish on Diamondback Energy, an independent oil and natural gas company focused on energy reserves that are primarily in the Permian Basin in West Texas. The company recently paid a base cash dividend of $1.10 per share for the second quarter of 2026. FANG stock offers a dividend yield of 2.2%.

Mehta increased his price target on FANG stock to $220 from $212 and reiterated a buy rating, citing the company's capital-efficient volume growth as oil prices remain high amid persistent supply disruption in the Middle East.

"We remain constructive on FANG, a high-quality, pure-play Permian Basin operator that continues to drive incremental capital efficiencies and strong well productivity relative to peers," said Mehta.

Also, the analyst said that the removal of a minimum return of capital commitment gives Diamondback more flexibility in taking capital allocation decisions amid high oil price volatility and macro uncertainty.

Mehta added that Diamondback sees a favorable environment for production growth, driven by the potential need for global inventory restocking. He highlighted that during Q2 2026, FANG's production volumes of 1,018 Mboe/d exceeded the high end of the company's guidance, driven by a stronger-than-expected natural gas output from Barnett development and improved downstream gas marketing.

Overall, Mehta is optimistic about Diamondback's growth and sees more upside in the stock, given higher production estimates, solid productivity and a deepening position in the Barnett shale formation. See Diamondback Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#2936. SES AI: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Software-driven accuracy gains could let battery customers buy less hardware, while M-Labs now targets manufacturers directly.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:01AM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 12, 2026, SES AI (SES -7.19%) announced a 25% sequential narrowing of its net loss to $13.4 million. It's pivoting from legacy energy storage hardware toward specialized defense and software markets where it owns production facilities. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Management is directing capital into drone battery manufacturing. The company completed the scale-up at its South Korean factory to satisfy National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA) compliance rules, which mandate non-Chinese defense supply chains.

Energy storage systems (ESS), which supply stationary battery power, generate nearly half of current revenue. SES AI uses Edgebox, its on-site software device, to calculate state-of-charge metrics far more accurately than legacy tools, letting customers purchase less physical hardware.

Molecular Universe, the company's battery material discovery platform, expanded into a stand-alone software product named M-Labs. Battery manufacturers evaluate the air-gapped system to accelerate testing without exposing proprietary customer data to external networks.

Demand for drone batteries is accelerating as defense supply chains pivot away from China. The company relies on its Korean factory, proprietary software, and $137 million in debt-free liquidity to scale production. Operational priorities center on commercial conversion rather than lab testing.

Combining software intelligence, drone battery manufacturing, and material discovery creates an integrated engine for growth. Converting qualification trials into long-term purchase orders remains the primary test for the business.

#2935. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2932. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

Vinh ranks No. 123 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 28.50%. See Marvell Technology Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2931. 3 Energy Stocks to Buy Before 2026 Ends That Could Help You Make a Fortune

These energy companies are positioned for long-term expansion.

By Jeff Siegel – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:05AM EST

10 stocks we like better than GE Vernova ›

GE Vernova (GEV -0.99%), NextEra Energy (NEE +0.43%), and Williams Companies (WMB -0.34%) are three energy stocks worth your consideration before 2026 ends. All three are benefiting from growing electricity demand, particularly from artificial intelligence, data centers, and expanding industrial activity. But each approaches that opportunity differently.

GE Vernova manufactures power generation and electrical equipment. NextEra develops renewable energy projects and operates one of America's largest utilities. Williams owns natural gas infrastructure that helps supply power plants and industrial customers.

Indeed, no stock can guarantee extraordinary returns, but recent financial results and long-term growth prospects offer compelling reasons to own them.

Image source: Image source: Getty Images.

GE Vernova has become one of the biggest beneficiaries of the growing demand for electricity infrastructure. The company manufactures natural gas turbines, electrical grid equipment, and wind turbines. And as utilities and technology companies scramble to secure additional power, demand for much of its equipment continues to accelerate.

In Q3, revenue reached $12.6 billion, increasing 27% year over year. Net income totaled $1.3 billion, while adjusted EBITDA reached $1.9 billion. And the company's order backlog is particularly impressive.

GE Vernova ended September with approximately $197 billion in backlog, an increase of $20 billion from the previous quarter. Its gas turbine business is especially strong. The company reported a 122-gigawatt gas power equipment backlog and slot reservations, with expectations of reaching at least 130 gigawatts by year-end.

Meanwhile, its Electrification segment generated $3.9 billion in quarterly revenue, up 51%. Management subsequently raised its full-year revenue guidance to between $46.5 billion and $47.5 billion, with projected free cash flow of $16 billion to $17 billion.

NextEra Energy offers a different way to profit from increasing electricity consumption. Through Florida Power & Light, the company operates one of the largest regulated utilities in the U.S., and its NextEra Energy Resources business develops and operates renewable energy projects, battery storage systems, and other energy infrastructure.

Third-quarter adjusted earnings reached $2.59 billion, or $1.24 per share, compared with $1.13 per share a year earlier. That's approximately 9.7% earnings-per-share growth. The renewable energy business is also expanding.

During the quarter, NextEra Energy Resources added 3.7 gigawatts of renewable energy and storage projects to its backlog, bringing the total to approximately 36.7 gigawatts. Battery storage accounted for 2.4 gigawatts of those new additions.

NextEra also expects adjusted earnings per share to grow at a compound annual rate of at least 8% through 2032 and is targeting the same growth rate from 2032 through 2035. And unlike many high-growth energy companies, NextEra pays a dividend.

The combination of renewable energy development, regulated utility earnings, and shareholder distributions could make NextEra attractive if you're seeking long-term compounding rather than dramatic short-term price swings.

There are risks, though, including higher financing costs, regulatory decisions, and the substantial capital required to develop new infrastructure. That said, NextEra still offers exposure to renewable energy without depending entirely on the success of an emerging technology.

Williams Companies doesn't manufacture turbines or develop solar farms. It owns natural gas pipelines, processing facilities, and related infrastructure. That business could become increasingly valuable as natural gas consumption rises alongside electricity demand.

Williams reported third-quarter adjusted EBITDA of approximately $2.04 billion, up 6% year over year. Operating cash flow increased 5% to $1.51 billion, while adjusted earnings per share rose 10% to $0.54. The company also completed its acquisition of Momentum Midstream, expanding its presence in the Haynesville region, a major natural gas-producing area supplying Gulf Coast markets.

Management now expects adjusted EBITDA growth of more than 11% annually through 2030. Williams also pays a dividend, giving you a source of income while the company pursues additional growth.

Of course, pipeline development faces regulatory hurdles, construction costs, and potential delays. Williams also carries significant debt, making financing costs worth monitoring. But unlike companies whose fortunes depend primarily on natural gas prices, Williams earns substantial revenue from transporting and handling the commodity. That is a significant strategic advantage.

I'd give GE Vernova the edge for aggressive growth investors, provided its valuation leaves room for reasonable returns. Its enormous backlog and accelerating equipment demand provide considerable earnings visibility.

NextEra Energy would be my choice if you're looking for renewable energy exposure, dividend income, and relatively predictable utility earnings. And Williams offers a compelling combination of natural gas infrastructure growth and income.

Ultimately, these companies aren't likely to make anyone rich overnight. But their expanding businesses could help investors build substantial wealth over the next decade. And with electricity demand expected to remain a major investment theme well beyond 2026, all three deserve consideration before the year ends.
